Sir:
For the Embassy's information I have the honor to
report the high lights of talk made by President Tasaki
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
of the Kobe University of Commerce at a private luncheon
APR2
lub in Kobe on March 9, 1938. The opinions expresad
OF STATE
A-M/C
by Dr. Tasaki, while by no means typical of informed
opinion among Japanese in Kobe, is illustrative of the
trend of thought among certain liberals who, although
way.
Dr. Tasaki chose as his subject the dedication of a
certain shrine on the Island of Kyushu to the momory of
FILED
Japanese war. He went out of his way to state that in
his opinion it was a matter of regret that General Nogi
and other military maders at the turn of the
Japan were not al ive today to shape the pallc

=== Page 20 === (confidence 0.98)
-2一
Japanese Army. He added that he believed that the
course of events on the Asiatic mainland since 1930
would have been far different from what i has proved
to be had those leaders survived until the present day.
During his rather discursive speech Dr. Tasaki
dwelt on the significance of the Imperial Rescript on
Education of the Emperor Meiji of October 30, 1890.
He ventured the assertion that he was satisfied that
the principles which dominated the Japanese government
today are substantially at variance with the precepts
laid down in this Imperial Rescript. He specifically
quoted three phrases from that Rescript which he believed
were violated by present Japanese international policies.
These were the injunctions to "advance public good",
"render illustrious the best traditions of the fore-
fathers", and follow the "way infallible in all ages
and true in all places".
The speaker declared that it was realized by the
Emperor Meiji that Japan's own interests would ultimately
be best served by following a policy of enlightened
internationalism; that a policy of disregarding the rights
and interests of other nations in any part of the world
would surely have an eventual adverse effect on Japan
herself; and that therefore in reality the selfish
interests of Japan in the international field coincided
with the most altruistic interpretation of her inter-
national obligations.

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
POLITICA
Foreign
If the year 1937 opened fcr Japan in an ominous key
it was largely due to the fact that the country was faced
with great uncertainty on every side, particularly in
respect of its relations with foreign countries and of
its position with respect to such world questions as, for
instance, naval armament. Many of the uncertainties with
which the country was faced last year have now been swept
away by the momentous events of l937, and Japan stands at
the threshold of 1938 facing perhaps the most serious out-
look in her modern histcry. Uncertainties have given way
to stark realities and the country is beset with grave
dangers both without and within.
The outstanding event of the year 1937 in East Asia
was, of course, the outbreak of the hostilities with China
which developed with disconcerting rapidity from a local
incident into militery cperations on a large scale, showing
nt the presert writing every indication of extending for an
indefinite period into the future. While the first phase
of these military operations may be said to be approaching
its close, following the fall of Nanking in December last,
there now stretches before the Japanese a long period fraught
With even greater uncertainty than that with which they
were faced at the beginning of 1937, in which the country's
utmost resources and energy will have to be taxed in order
to consolidate the present precarious position in China
so deerly bought by force of arms. Whatever may be the
opinion

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.99)
opinion of the rest of the werli, there is no doubt but
that the Japanese themselves are firmly convinced of the
innate rightness of the cause for which they are fighting
in China; they feel that they are undertaking a task in
that country which no one else wished to undertake, namely,
that of bringing order out of chaos and of forcing the un-
reliable Chinese to permit foreign countries to trade and
prosper in that country in an orderly fashinn, unhempered
by the vagaries of irresponsible local officials not responsive
to the authority of the Central Government. It is the be-
lief of the Jepanese that in view of the fact that other
countries will not assist them in this task of rehabilitating
China, the powers should at least permit the Japanese to
perform without let or hindrance what they believe to be
their duty.
Japan's relations with the world in general, and
especially with those powers which have interests in the
Pacific area, have greatly suffered as the result of the
China venture, and there appears little likelihood that
these relations will improve in the neer future.
Relations with Great Britein are perhaps strained
to a greater degree than those with any other country,
and there is a firm conviction in the minds of meny
Japanese that Great Britein is Japan's real enemy behind
the scenes in China.
Relations with the United States, however, have, so
far as Japan is concerned, continued surprisingly favor-
able despite the series of shocks which they have been
called upon to withstand as a result of the ruthless
actions

=== Page 30 === (confidence 0.97)
-5-
actions of the Japenese military in dameging Americen right..
interests, and property in China.
Japan's relations with Soviet Russia heve become seriou.
ly stroined, as was to be uxpected, by reason of the attitude
of Soviet Russia in the China conflict and of the material
and moral assistance rendered to the Chiang Kai-shek Govern-
ment by the Soviets. Cront as is this strain, however, few
signs have been apparent on the surface due to Japen's pres-
ent policy of avoiding the risk of a clnsh with her henvily
armed neighbor, at lurst until the Chine situation is well
in hand, and to the obvicus unwillingness of Soviet Russia
to fight Japan unluss attacked by her.
While there re few cutstending controversial questions
of e noture which might c use friction butween Jopan and the

G e r m a ny
At the beginning of 1937 relations between Japan and Germeny
were extremely cordial, and in fact it may be noted that Germany
was the only Occidental country with which Japan hed succeeded in
concluding a political agreement since she became an "outcast"
nation by virtue of the Manchurian incident of l93l. When Japan
signed the anti-comintern pact with Germany in November 193s,
Japan took the first step toward emerging from the state of vir-
tual isolatibn which followed her abandonment of the League of
Nations in March of 1934. At the time the anti-comintern agree-
ment was signed there was much speculation as to the possibility
of the existence of a secret clause providing for the exchange
of military information and for the supply of arms and ammunition
and technical aid to Japan; however, since the outbreak of the
hostilities with China there has been no evidence to support the
view that such a secret clause existed or that, in any case, even
if it did exist it was being put into effect.
The German situation vis-à-vis Japan during the current
China affair has become increasingly delicate, and while perhaps
both countries are satisfied with certain political aspects of
the axis, which has since been strengthened by the adherence of
Italy, nevertheless it has become more and more apparent that
German sympathies have not been entirely with Japan. In fact,
they have been perhaps more with China. It is significant that
the German military and technical advisers to Chiang Kai-shek
have continued to function throughout the hostilities, while on
the other hand when Italy joined the Berlin-Tokyo axis she
immediately withdrew all her aviation experts from China and came
out wholeheartedly in support of Japan.

Nether1ands
Relations between Japan and the Netherlands, like those
between Japan and other Occidental countries with interests in
the Far East, have suffered as a result of the present hostilities
with China. During the first six months of 1937 some progress
was made toward the elimination of the ever-present trade and
economic difficulties between Japan and the Netherlands East
Indies; at the end of February, Mr. Kuwashima, the newly ap-
pointed Japanese Minister to The Hague, departed en route to
his post by way of the East Indies for the purpose of making
a tour of investigation. This was the first time that a
Japanese Minister accredited to the Netherlands had been ordered
to familiarize himself with the situation in the East Indies
before proceeding to his post, and upon the conclusion of his
tour Mr. Kuwashima indicated that he had profited by his inves-
tigations and predicted the successful conclusion of the trade
negotiations then in progress. His predictions were fulfilled
when on April 9, 1937, the trade controversy which had existed
since May l934 was settled by the signature of a provisional
trade agreement between Japan and the Netherlands East Indies
Government. The principal provision was that 25 per cent of
imports into the East Indies from Japen were to be handlod by
Japanese importers and merchants on the condition thet Japan was
to purchase Java sugar on a prefential basis. The agreement
also included a cleuse by which Japan agreed to give favorable
treatment to such Netherlands East Indies products as coffee,
tobacco, and resin. Early in June three Netherlands East Indies
delegates errived in Japen and held provisionel conversations
at Osake in an endeavor to work out some agreement in accordance
with

=== Page 54 === (confidence 0.99)
-29-
with the foregoing clause. These conversations ended in July,
however, without agreement since the two delegations were
unable to agree concerning the apportionment of quotas for
Japanese: exports to the Netherlands East Indies betwecn
Japanese exporters and Netherlands East Indies exporters in
Japan.
So much for commercial affairs. The outbreak of hostili-
ties between Japan and China brought sharply to the fore the
political differences with The Hague and later developments
have been viewed by the Dutch with growing and grave concern.
Ever since the Ameu statement of April 1934, when the Japanese
announced to the world their policy of hegemony in East Asia,
and the later (1935) development of the "Southward edvance"
policy of the Jepanese navy, the Netherlands Government has
been increesingly egiteted concerning the danger inherent in
the situetion with regard to the safety of the East Indies.
The keystone of Dutch policy hes been close cooperation with
Greet Britain end reliance upon the British nevy for protection.
The present Sino-Japenese effeir, however, has developed to a
certain degree into an attack upon British interests end upon
British prestige in East Asia to the consequent detriment of
Dutch interests in the same sphere. With the British position
threetened in the East end weakened in Europe and elsewhere,
the time is rapidly approeching when the Dutch will be forced
to revise their policy of hiding behind the skirts of the British
fleet and will have to arm themselves to protect their East
Indies. I believe thet steps in this direction are alreedy under
contempletion and that the Netherlands East Indies forces are to
be strengthened with the addition of aircraft, submarines, and
other forms of defensive weapons.

I t a 1 y
In this day of rapidly changing political realignments
there are perhaps no stranger bedfellows than Japan and Italy.
These two countries, whose newly-found friendship is heing
broadcast to the world at the present time, have no bonds of
culture, sentiment, commerce, or other ties which might
normally lead to a rarprochement, and their present close,
though artificial, relationship is due to a set of extraor-
dinary circumstances which have arisen not only as a result
of the Japanese campairn in on:na, but of conditions in
Europe, especially those irvolving Great Britain.
frior to and during the first nine months of 1937 rela-
tions between Japan and Italy were on a purely formal and
imnersonal rasis such as would normally exist hetween two
povers whose spheres of interest were not in any way con-
tiguous. It is true that mutual interest was aroused to a
slight degree hy the fact that both countries had left the
League or Mations, and that they were generally in disfavor
with the rest of the
world through a common tendency to dis-
regard treaty obligations and to have recourse to force in the
pursuit of their respective national policies. Similarly, they
were outstanding members of that group loosely described by
writers on internaticnal affairs as the "have not" countries.
It is also true that at the time of Italy's invasion of Ethio-
pia there was in evidence a certain amount of Japanese sym-
pathy and understanding for this venture. Nevertheless, it
is to be recalled that from the outbreak of the Sino-Japanese
hostilities and until the middle of October the Italian Gov-
ernment pursued a policy of scrupulous neutrality, and if there
was any sympathetic feeling on the part of the Italian public

While it may he arpued that the close relations between

The real bond which these two

Domestio
The year 1937 opened with domestic politics in a tur-
bulent state. The Hirota Cabinet which had come into cffice
after the February 26 incidert of the previous year had just
about run its course, and in fact resigned on January 23.
The political struggle was a three-cornered one, with the
political parties well out of hand and in full opposition
to the Cabinet; at the same time the Cabinet itself was
suffering from an attempt of the Army to dominate it, which
in fact it eventually succeeded in doing.
When the Diet. which was in recess for the usual New
Years holidays, reconvened on January 2l, the political
parties, led by the Minseito and Seiyukai, resumed their
opposition to the Government and attacked the Cabinet and
the military for their interference in politics. The Diot
vas immediately prorogued for two days, and on January 23
the Cabinet resigned rather than yield to the Army's demands
that the Diet be dissolved. Ceneral Ugaki was then requested
to form a Cabinet, but he was unable to do so in the face of
the Army's veto. The task was next offered to Baron Hiranuma,
Vice President of the Frivy Council, who was reported to have
fascist tendencies, but he refused to accept the responsi-
bility. Finally. General Hayashi, who had been War Minister
in the ill-fated Okada Cabinet whose demise was brought about
by the February 26 incident, was selected, and with the sup-
port of the Army succeeded in forming a Cabinet which was
installed on February 22.
Although on the face of it the choice of Hayashi was a
seeming victory for the Army, nevertheless it must be recog-
nized that Hayashi was distinctly a compromise candidate and

which comments upon the significance
of the unconcealed disunity in the
Japanese Cabinet, 1s exceptionally in-

According to the best information
available, Konoye is the leader of a

The Embassy's analysis of the situation
appears to be well reasoned and sound, and
so far has been borne out by the increasing
vigor of Japanese military operations.

1784
I have the honor to refer to my telegram 239, April
 p.m., in which I reported that differences of opinion
with regard to important policies had developed within the
786
38
Cabinet, and to my telegram 282, April 28, 7 p.m., in
hi
the opinion was expressed that these differences had
parently
been tided over.
Although the internal political situetion has been out-
wardly calm ever since the outbreak in July last of the
hostilities
F/F

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.98)
6238
一 2-
hostilities with China, there have been a number of under-
currents indicating unrest: the political parties have beem
increasingly conscious of the futility of their opposition
to the revival of bureaucracy, business elements have been
intensely unhappy over the trend toward governmental con-
trol of industry and commerce, and among all classes there
exists bewilderment with regard to the failure of Japanese
operations in China to unfold more rapidly and successfully
than has been the case. In spite of the extreme importance
of maintaining national unity, and, further, of maintaining
semblances of national unity, and in spite of the absolute
power of those in authority to suppress all dissentient
elements, the fact is significant that the existence of
disunity within the Cabinet, the supreme directive and
administrative agency of the Government, could not be
concealed. The purpose of the present despatch is to
estimate the significance of that fact.
The Prime Minister is not a bureaucrat, a militarist,
a member of any political party, or a businessman. In this
lies his strength, for his lack of affiliation with any of
these groups has made him acceptable to all classes and
groups. With Prince Konoye in the Cabinet are (aside from
the Ministers of War and Navy, whose attitudes are always
determined by the respective services) the Home Minister,
Admiral Suyetsugu, a military firebrand, Minister of Agri-
culture and Forestry Count Arima, an aristocrat and a close
friend of Konoye's, Minister of Overseas Affairs Count
otani,

=== Page 70 === (confidence 0.99)
6239
-3 -
A Otani, the head of a noble ecclesiastical family, Education
Minister Marquis Kido, a bosom friend of Prince Konoye's, and
a "string" of other ministers, all bureaucrats, some with
party affiliations which are no longer important. We find
then a cabinet of bureaucrats and friends of the Prime Minister,
headed by a man without special obligations to any poli-
tical faction.
The Government as thus constituted, aside from a minor
change made last yeer, has administered the affairs of the
nation througin nine months of conflict with China. It has
laid down as a fundamental policy that it has "ceased to
deal with" the Wationalist Government of China. It has
succeeded in the recent session of the Diet in securing the
passage in substantially the forn it desired of a large number
of laws, some of them, especially the National Mobilization
Law and the Electric Power Control Law, far-reaching in their
ultimate effect on the Japanese political and social structure.
Notwithstanding these accomplishments, to which should be
added a fair measure of military success, doubt and appre-
hension with regard to future political developments appeared,
and at the time of writing have not been entirely removed.
The recent Cabinet crisis - for lack of a more precise
term - was precipitated by an observation, whether casual or
made with calculated effect, by Prince Konoye that because
of ill-health anü other personal reasons he wished to resign
the premiership. There then occurred a scurrying to-and-fro
of important political figures - a sure sign in this country
that

=== Page 71 === (confidence 0.99)
6240
- 4 -
that serious political diffioulties have arisen. Under
banner headlines, the press took notice of the politioal
crisis, but the voluminous disoussions of the situation
were sheer froth and gave no plausible explanation. It
would have been entirely too ingenious to believe that the
situation had arisen over Prince Konoye's physioal condition,
which was widely stressed, for the reason that Prinoe Konoye
has not been in robust health for several years. It was
being repeatedly affirmed by his friends that Prince Konoye
has no political ambitions; and from what we know of his
personality and character we are inclined to agree with
that statement. If ill-health were a controlling factor,
it would be reasonable to suppose that Prince Konoye would
have actually resigned immediately after the session of the
Diet, through which the Government carried through its legis-
lative program practically intact, thenks largely to the
efforts of Prince Konoye himself. Further, it must be
assumed that no one realized more clearly than did Prince
Konoye that the situation confronting Japan, both internally
and externally, was not one which would allow him to resign
for reasons of personal convenience. In the light of these
considerations, it was obvious that the controlling factor
in the situation was the question whether differences of
opinion among those sharing in the execution of fundamental
policies could be adjusted.
Among certain observers, the view was held that the
crisis was caused by the insistence of a certain group
within the Cabinet that the National Mobilization Law be
immediately

=== Page 72 === (confidence 0.98)
6241
- 5 -
immediately invoked. In the light of the formal assuranee
which the Government gave to the Diet that the law would
not be applied during the period of the present hostili-
ties in China, the reports which we had reoeived in con-
fidence from Japanese friends of a virtual oleavage
over the carrying out of the Chine policy seemed to be
a more correct estimate of the cause of the crisis. The
latter view has been largely borne out by subsequent events.
As previously stated, the policy of the Japanese
Government is to "cease to deal with the Nationelist Govern-
ment". It is obvious that this phrase describes merely
the negative aspect of Japan's policy, the declaration
of January loth in which the phrase occurs being singularly
uninformative on the affirmative aspects of policy and on
the methods to be employed in their prosecution. It would
be to beg the question merely to say that Japan seeks
to destroy the Chinese Government by methods of force.
There need to be considered such guestions as the number
of troops to be sent to China, the area of operations ,
purchase from abroad of supplies, maintenance of national
credit, and a thousand and one other questions. Some of
these problems fall within the jurisdiction or responsibility
of the Government, while others fall entirely within the
jurisdiction of Imperial Headquarters. It is to be sup-
posed that the coordination of what might be desirable
from a military point of view with diplomatic, financial
and economic realities is in itself a fundamental problem.
In

=== Page 73 === (confidence 0.98)
6242
- 6 -
In theory satisfactory liaison between the two agencies is
assured by specially appointed officers and, for a period
of several months, by frequent meetings between officers
of Imperial Headquarters and certain members of the Cabinet
who formed an "Inner Cabinet" - the Prime Minister, the
Foreign Minister, the War and Navy Ministers and the Minister
of Finance. Nevertheless, according to reliable sources,
there has been a fundamental difference of opinion between
that element of the Cabinet which follows Prince Konoye
and Imperial Headquarters over the guestion of methods
which should be used to bring the conflict with China
to a successful conclusion.
The best information available is that Prince Konoye
leads a group, including r. irota and Marquis Kido,
which advocates the urgent need of bringing the conflict
to a successful conclusion and whose conception of strategy,
k.,Koichu
both military and politicel, is that it should be elastic
and opportunistic. The War Minister, reflecting the opinion
of Imperial Headquarters, is a proponent of the view that
pressure steadily exerted on China, by military operations
within limited key areas and by economic and financiel
measures, will eventually bring about the collapse of
Chinese resistance. At first glance, such an alignment
would seem to be paradoxical; but it is in fact not im-
probable. There is every reason why the Army, with a
powerful potential foe on its right flank, would prefer to
husband

=== Page 74 === (confidence 0.98)
6243
- 7 -
husband its resources as much as possible and avoid the rim
inherent in dissipating its strength by conduoting operations
throughout the length and breadth of China. On the other
hand, it can be reasonably assumed that Prinoe Konoye and
his supporters are more alive than is the Army to the
risks of involvement with Great Britein and the United
States, and to the threat to Japan's materiel well-being,
the longer the conflict continues.
Whatever satisfaction Prince Konoye can derive from
the basic soundness of his position he cannot blink the
fact that, in enjoying thus far the support of the Army,
he remains in office only so long as it may please the
Army to have him there. Knowing something of Prinoe Konoye,
I am of the opinion that he would not have remained in the
intolerable position of acting as a figurehead for the Army
and that, notwithstanding a fundamental difference of opinion
with the Army as to method, there existed until the orisis
developed some common ground. It is interesting to note in
retrospect that the Cabinet difficulties first became known
immediately after the Japanese military reverse in Southern
Shantung. In any event, General Sugiyama, the War Minister,
00F
made a literally flying visit to China to inspect condi-
3.,Gen
tions at the several fronts, Larquis Kido consulted the
Elder Statesman, Prince Saionji, and Prince Konoye duly
announced that he would continue in office. There are
suggestions in the press that the military operations will
be conducted hereafter with greater vigor, and, although
no official statement to that effect has been made, an
advance on Hankow is believed by many intelligent Japanese
to

=== Page 75 === (confidence 0.97)
6244
- 8-
to be in preparation. I cannot support my estimate by
quotation from authoritative sources, as no person in
authority would permit himself to be approached on this
subjeot; but it is my belief, as stated in my telegrams
under reference, that there was a serious difference of
opinion among those in control with regard to what should
be done by Japan in China and that this difference has been
tided over, presumably by the making of a limited concession
by the Army to the Prime Minister.
No useful forecast of future trends can now be made.
Excluding the two Service members, the two most forceful
individuals in the Cabinet are Marquis Kido, the Minister of
Education, and Admiral Suyetsugu, the Home Minister. Marquis
Kido is extremely well-informed and a definite realist. He
is very close to the Prime Minister and is one of his most
trusted and active advisers. Admiral Suyetsugu, on the other
hand, is a visionary, and an active advocate of totalitarian
doctrine. For Prince Konoye, it is no difficult or unpalatable
task to accept Liarquis Kido's advice and maintain a position
of independence, while it does not seem to be "in" Prince
Konoye to become the mouthpiece of such fanaticism as that
with which the name of Suyetsugu has become associated. There
are indications that Prince Honoye, in standing out against
the Army, has won at least a partial victory and that the
Army has "liked it". I am inclined to the view that the part-
nership will continue for some time.
Respectfully yours,
soh.F
800.
Joseph C. Grew.

I have the honor to summarize below the press
comment on the recent changes in the Japanese Cabinet.
The Times says that the only deduction which can be
made is that the Japanese wilitarists are preparing to
fight to a finish.
It thinks the immediate cause was
a serious dispute that arose over the powers of the
"Chinese Affairs Bureau" which was set up to direot
F/FG
Japanese policy in those parts of China which are under
Japanese

=== Page 94 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
Japanese military occupation.
The Foreign Minister
wished to bring this Bureau under the control of his
Department; the Army opposed this plan; and the
Prime Minister, who preferred to place the Bureau under
direct control of the Cabinet, won the support of the
military by undertaking to include two more generals
in his reconstituted Ministry.
The Daily Telegraph and Morning Post points out
that Prince Konoye has hitherto refused to yield to the
a
agitation against Mr. Hiroto and believes that his
present surrender is an indication of the formidable
pressure to which he has been exposed.
This is ominous,
and means that the governing power of control has now
definitely passed into the hends of the fighting services.
The Daily Telegraph and Morning Post regards it as certain
that the temper of the war chiefs and the mood of the
people have become more rather than less bellicose and
that this fect has to be reckoned with.
Always it is
the wounded animal thet is most dangerous.
The Liberal Manchester Guardian says that though the
Japanese armed forces have still a long way to go before
they conquer China, they seem already to have conquered
Japan, and there is no doubt that the changes represent a
definite move toward the peculiar brand of military fascism
to which Japan has long been tending.
Referring to the
battle of Suchow, it says that from the beginning of the
war it has been the great ambition of the Japanese staff
to encircle and annihilate the Chinese armies.
This
battle

=== Page 95 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
battle provided the best opportunity for such a stroke
but the Japanese failed to make their circle complete.
The Guardian thinks that clearly there is no likelihood
of a Chinese collapse.
There is nothing for it but to
push on into the interior after the retreating armies,
with communications growing ever longer, with more terri-
tory to garrison, and with less hope of success.
The Liberal News-Chronicle is not certain that the
ultra-military section of the Army has triumphed, and
points out that General Ugaki and Mr. Ikeda figured on
the list of people to be murdered by the "young officers"
movement.
If and when these elderly Ministers fail the
turn of the young "patriots" will come, and then the stage
may be set for the climax of the tragedy of Japan.
Respectfully yours,
For the Ambassador:
Herschel V. Johnson
Counselor of Embassy

Moscow's no. 1291 of May 18, 1938, en-
titled "Soviet comment on internal conflict
in Japan."
The despatch paraphrases an article from
the Pacific Star of Khabarovsk, enclosed in
full, on internal political conflict in Japan.
The Soviet writer sees in the parliamentary
opposition to the national general mobiliza-
tion bill passed during the 73d session of the
Japanese liet an underlying conflict between
the Jananese Cabinet and the more "far-sighted"
elements of the "bourgeoisie" and predlcts that
sharper differences of opinion may be expected
between the majority of the Diet members and
the Government. The conclusion is drawn "that
the restless atmosnhere in the Japanese parlia-
ment clearly bears witness to the failure of the
attempts of tle militarists to reach 'national
unity' on the basis of war." The writer asserts
that the struggle has only aided the further
sharponing of class contracictions within Japan.
He concludes with the familiar charge that the
Japanese militarists are planning an attack on
the Soviet Union.
Comment: The conclusion that the politi-
cal dissension in the 73d session of the Diet
"bears witness to the failure of the attempts
of

=== Page 97 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
of the militarists to reach 'national unity'
on the basis of war" is not warranted, nor
is the assertion that the struggle has
heightened class contradictions. The political
dissension in t: e 73d session of the Diet
should be interpreted in the light of the fact
that the political narties have been completely
discredited in Japan: They no longer have
roots in any part of the Japanese hody politic
and their opposition in the 73d session was a
death struggle to prevent complete extinction
at the hands of a rapidly developing totali-
tarian state. Class contradictions have not
been heightened but have been lessened by the
present conflict. Both the textile manufacturer
and the farmer are convinced of the righteous-
ness of the Ja anese cause in China and sre
united in their efforts to see it to a finish.

Tokyo's No. 2975 of May 31, 1938, en-
titled "Changes Within The Konoe Cabinet",
which pays reading in full, consists of a
review of the new personalities in the
Japanese Cabinet together with brief ex-
planations for the recent Cabinet changes.
General Ugaki is described as bring-
ing to the Cabinet long administrative
experience as a military man in important
political office. The Embassy feels that
some more concentrated and centralized
machinery for dealing with China may be
created and that Ugaki has been chosen for
this reason. The selection of Mr. Seihin
Ikeda, a Mitsui man, to hold both the Com-
merce and Industry and Finance portfolios
is seen by the Embassy not as a reversion
toward free big business enterprise but as
a change in method: bureaucratic controls
are to remain but a businessman is to carry
them out. Araki's entry into the Cabinet
is regarded as an answer to the need of the
War Ministry for someone competent to carry
out an "educational" campaign in support
of the hostilities. Itagaki's inclusion
in the Cabinet is predicted and considerable
discussion is given to the question whether
the

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
the reversion to Kwantung army leadership
means the positive, enerretic, decisive
prosecution of the present conflict or a
shifting of the focus of Japanese interest
back to north China even at the cost of
activity in central China. The despatch
concludes with a statement that the changes
within the Konoe Cabinet have served to
strengthen the Government and that these
changes seem to the Embassy to imply an
increasingly positivist pursuit of the
current hostilities in China.
Conlent: The question of the creation
of a central or~an, separate from the For-
eign Office, for dealing with north China
is given here for the first time the value
it deserves. Its si nificance appears to
have been obscured by surface movements,
but with Ugaki in the Cabinet there is every
reason to believe that the plan will reach
fruition in a short time.

Sin:
In despatch 2912 of May 2, 1938 I had the honor to
report as of that date the Embassy's analysis of the political
situation then prevailing, which had given rise to consideration
of reorganization of the Japanese Cabinet. It now appears
that such reorganization was continuously under consideration
and disoussion, along the lines of that despatoh, and that
recent developments have been simply the maturing and fruition
of the process. By May 24 (Embassy's telegram 330,May 25,
7 p.m.) it became clear that the matter was coming to head.
Prince
n

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.97)
- 2 -
Prince Konoe has remained premier, but important changes
within his cabinet have been made from May 26 to May 30
(Embassy's telegrams 332, May 26, 8 p.m.; 333, May 26,
midnight; 335, May 27, 6 p.m.; 336, May 28, 5 p.m.).
General Kazushige Ugaki has become foreign minister; Mr.
Seishin Ikeda has become finance minister and concurrently
minister of commerce and industry; General Sadao Araki has
become minister of education. The vacancies filled by these
appointments were created by the resignation of Mr. Hirota
from the foreign ministership, Mr. Kaya from the office
of minister of finence, and Mr. Yoshino from the office
of minister of commerce and industry, and by the withdrawal
of iarquis Koichi Kido from the office of minister of education,
although
/retaining he office of minister of health. These appoint-
ments announced on May 26. On May 30 the Government announced
the appointment of Lieutenant General Eiki Tojo as vice
minister of war. At the same time there are persistent
reports from credible sources that General Sugiyama is
shortly to resign the war ministership, to be sucoeeded
probably by Lieutenant General Seishiro Itagaki, possibly
by Lieutenant General Kuniaki Koiso.
I.
√
General Kazushige Ugaki brings to the foreign minister-
ship long administrative experience as a military man in
1
important political office. Four times in the past he has
been war minister, and his high political ambition, in
conflict with others, led to an impasse which resulted in
1931 in his nomination as governor general of Chosen to
remove

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.98)
D
- 3 -
remove him from the immediate political stage. This office
he held until the summer of 1936. In January 1937 when the
Hirota Cabinet fell, Ugaki was asked to form a cabinet, but
failed because he was deliberately blocked by the army, which
refused to supply a war minister, without which Ugaki was
unable to go ahead(Embassy's despatch 2241, February 1, 1937).
This was a bitter experience, and won for Ugaki general
popular sympathy. He was brought back from retirement by
appointment as cabinet adviser in October l937 when that
office was created as a consequence of the China conflict
and was filled by a nuaber of prominent men, more or less
paired conservative and radical in the various important
categories from which named. General Ugaki seemed to go
in in the capacity of a conservative military man balancing
General Araki as a radical military man.
Ugaki's appointment as foreign minister seems to be
for the purpose of bringing to the government a first class
administrative mind. That some more concentrated and cen-
tralized machinery of directing Japanese polioy in China
will be attempted suems an imminent probability, and such
a trend will demand of the ministry of foreign affairs a
degree of adaptation in which Ugaki's administrative and
organizing genius will be of great service. As one new to
that ministry he is in position to proceed with necessary
changes unhampered by personal commitments and under-
standings carried over from ordinary times. He brings also
wide popular support and the support of an important
minority in the army. It appears that he is fully reconciled
to prosecution of the conflict, and is prepared to accept
without

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.97)
-4 -
without reservation the policy advocated by Premier Konoe.
The same apparently can not be said of Hirota.
COF
Hirota once said, and later reiterated, that there
H
kakc
would be no war during his holding of office. It is possibly
worth noting that Ugaki comes to his task free of any such
underteking.
The ease with which Ugaki has been accepted as foreign
minister by the military offers striking contrast to the
ignominious blocking which he suffered at the hands of the
same military in the closing days of January l937 in his
attempt to form a cabinet. The passage of but sixteen
months might be thought quite inadequate to consummate so
sweeping & change of heart. But there are differences which
make the present more palatable. For one thing the present
project is not to turn over to Ugaki the formation of a
cabinet in which process his own views must be the dominant
note in the defining of a new policy. Secondly, his appear-
ance in the cabinet is amply balanced by others of advanced
army views , and the public can not possibly receive the
impression that the army is losing ground in the government.
Thirdly, these are times of all-important hostilities,
Ugaki's ability can be of service, his popular following
can add appreciably to active support by the people for the
government. Fourthly, the very existence of the conflict,
a triumph of planning over a number of years by the military,
is adequate safeguard against innovations feared from Ugaki
sixteen months ago and now utterly impossible.
The statement is freely hazarded, both in the press
and among important Japanese, that General Ugaki will become
premier

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
premier within a short time through resignation of Prince
Konoe by reason of health. The prevalence of this expectation
is proof of thorough support on the part of Ugaki for what
are deemed to be the necessities of Japanese policy in the
present hostilities, and of a reconciliation with the military
which removes the one previous obstacle to the use of this
able man's talents. Admittedly the present cabinet changes are
such that Ugaki could very conceivably slip into the premier-
ship almost automatically.
II.
Mr. Seihin Ikeda, newly named to be minister of commerce
11
and industry and minister of finance, is a man of seventy
with long practical business experience in Mitsui followed
by the governorship of the Bank of Japan. Born in 1867, he
graduated from Keio University, went to Harvard for the study
of economics, took up work on the Jiji newspaper upon return
to Japan, shortly changed to the Mitsui Benk, rose rapidly,
and when Baron Takuma Dan was assassinated in 1932 succeeded
to the highest Mitsui position as chief managing director of the
holding company, Mitsui Gomei Kaisha. His first duty was to
bring about the changes in Mitsui which he deemed called for
by modern Japanese developments in politico-social ideas.
In 1937, upon the nomination of Foreign Minister Yuki, he
served e few months as governor of the Bank of Japan, then
resigned because of ill health. His health is still question-
able.
In October 1937 Ikeda became a cabinet adviser as a
representative of business and finance, paired with Baron Goh.
Baron

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6-
Baron Goh is a conservative, and it is to be presumed that
Ikeda, in this appointment, was recognized as being of
advanced ideas.
Ikeda's succession to Kaya as finance minister is not a
reversion toward free big business enterprise as if in
acknowledgment of the failure of control. It is a change
in the direction of methods more acceptable to business men
without change in principles. Kaya, always a bureaucrat,
has never had any practical experience, and Ikeda has had
plenty of it; but Ikeda's statenents to the press (Embassy's
telegram 336, May 28, 5 p.m.) have made it amply clear that
Kaya's same controls will continue in effect. The disquieting
irreducible r quirements of Japanese finance and trade in the
present state of af airs leave very little lat tude of decision.
The new minister says that, as before, the yen will continue
to be held at one shilling and two pence, the only gold
to be exported will be newly mined gold, the 8ol million
yen gold currency backing will not be touched, effort will
be made to relax import restrictions on essential primary
commodities. Meantime the only new factors which offer
possibility of practical help are the joining of the two
ministries under a single minister, holding out some hope
of better administering of control by reason of coordination;
and the fact of long experience on the part of Ikeda with the
practical problems of business. In these factors,it should
be recorded, the business community seems unquestionably to
find comfort and hope. The prospeot is of relief in method,
not in principles.
The

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.97)
-7-
The effort to appease opposition and to give confidence
to business by this cabinet change does not present to the
candid observer much prospect of success. The costs of the
conflict are dirficult to produce, and the process must become
more and more disliked by the people. A change of finance
minister may serve temporarily to allay misgivings; but the
effect can hardly last long, and it is probable that changes
in minister will hereafter have to be made from time to time
in a futile effort to make the pill less bitter.
III.
General Araki brings to the government, in the present
emergency, a name which still has its force among many of the
most fervent officers of the army. In the common effort
A.
to sink differences after the insurgency of February 26,
l936 he was retired and withdrew from the stage of affairs.
Now, with great difficulties still facing Japan in China,
it would appear that the government sees advantage in
again bringing his faction into influence. of Araki's
personal qualifications for direction of an "educational"
campaign in support of the hostilities (and such is the
current definition of the urgent work of the ministry) there
is no question
IV.
Lieutenant General Eiki Tojo has become vice minister of
war; and the report that Lieutenant General Seishiro Itagaki
Toj'
will shortly become war minister is so persistent that it can
not be ignored. Tojo until recently was chief of staff of the
Eiki
Kwantung army in Manchuria. Itagaki is well known as a leading
officer of the Kwantung army, one of the four most important
engineers

=== Page 117 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
engineers of the Manchurian coup in 1931 and of Japanese
policy in Manchuria thereafter (Itagaki, Ishihara, Doihara,
Koiso). Jer Minister Sugiyama, on the other hand, is simply
a non-factional regular, brought into office as part of the
61
effort to submerge the conflict of army factions which mani-
fested itself so threateningly on February 26, 1936.
What this reversion to Kwantung army leadership may mean
in terms of future policy in China is far from obvious.
The Kwantung army has been in the forefront of those with a
positive plan of Japanese expansion on the continent of Asia,
and has formulated specific schemes of Jananese control of China.
Now, with the conflict with China in process, it is likely that
the positive, energetic, decisive prosecution of that conflict
appeals to such leaders as may be c ming to the war ministry
from the Kwantung army. But it is necessary to make mention
of another possible though unplausible interpretation in terms
quite contrary to this positivist interpretation. The Kwantung
army is known to have centered its pre-incident plar n north
China, the Mongolian frontier, the separation of China from the
Soviet Union, and always the Soviet Union as inevitable foe;
and it is also known that the Kwantung army has never been
convinced that the campaign which Japan launched in the Yangtze
valley in August 1937 was wise. However unlikely, it it not
beyond the bounds of possibility that a reassertion of Kwan-
tung army leadership would seek to shift the focus of Japanese
activity back to north China and the locus of the pre-incident
plan, even at the cost of economy of activity in central China.
Ugaki's

=== Page 118 === (confidence 0.95)
-9-
V.
Ugaki's advent as foreign minister suggests a change in
01
the vice ministership as also likely. Shiratori is playing
hard for the position, looking forvard to moving into the
ministership when Ugaki moves up as premier. The press speaks
of Ugaki as himself preferring Saito from Washington. Tani,
ambassador at large in China, is also mentioned. Horinouchi might
.j
go to Washington, Saito go to London, Yoshida retire, according
to one press opinion. All this remains in the realm of speculation.
VI.
The changes within the Konoe Cabinet have served to strengthen
the government and seem to the Embassy to imply an increasingly posi-
tivist pursuit of the current hostilities in China. Further
extension of the sphere of fighting appears destined, with operations
in south China to be included, even at the risk of conflict with
British interests. Prince Konoe has emerged the victor in establish-
ing a cabinet for the carrying out of his views, and because of
the balanced representation of groups constituting the nation he
is assured of maximum support for his policies. The way is cleared
for a more intensive prosecution of policy on the continent.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 148 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
ADS
75
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
July 21, 1938.
Reference two reports: Tokyo's No. 3000,
June 8, 1938, "Finance Minister Ikeda's Poli-
cies", and Tokyo's No. 3032, June 21, 1938,
"Import-Export Control Amendment".
When Mr. Ikeda became Minister of Fi-
nance in Japan in May 1938, he proceeded to
revise the then existing methods of trade
control. Since September 1937 trade and
exchange control had been enforced directly
by the Government and there had been many
complaints from business against the in-
elasticity of the administretive restric-
tions by the Government offices which had
been hastily organized after the beginning
of hostilities. Mr. Ikeda immediately pro-
posed, and he is carrying out those proposals,
to place more responsibility for the en-
forcement of the Government's trade regu-
lations in the hands of the trade guilds and
trade associations. The Embessy indioates
that this step is a wise and a logical one,
considering the importance of guilds and
trade associations in Japan's history.
Mr. Ikeda also aimed at the allevia-
tion of import restrictions on essential
raw materials as an inducement to the ex-
pansion of exports, and he has announced
that it is his desire to link importation
of raw materials with the exportation of
products made from those materials. (This
policy is being carriel out at the present
time.)
The

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
The Embassy coaments that "it is likely
that Japan's economic structure will emerge
from the China conflict permanently altered
in some of the ways which we think of as
fascist."
Mr. Ikeda is attempting to reduce con-
sumption of goods by the people through en-
couraging widespread saving and is attempt-
ing to discourage exports to Manchuria and
north China because exports to regions in
the yen area make no contribution to Japan's
foreign exchange.
The second report under reference deals
witn the implementation of mr. lkeda's poli-
cies as outlined above. as of ay 25 the
Japanese Government proclaimed ordinances
authorizing the creation of supply and demand
councils and laid down rules governing their
funotioning (the Embassy encloses transla-
tions of the pertinent portion of the law and
the ordinances in question).
The cotton industry, because of its im-
portance in Japan's economy and because of
the particularly difficult position in which
it has been placed by the stringent restric-
tions on imports, has been the first to or-
ganize a supply and demand council. The
council represents all phases of the cotton
industry, raw cotton importers, spinners,
manufactirers of all types of cotton textiles,
and exporters.
Later

=== Page 150 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
Later reports on this subject indicate
that 300,000,000 yen more of the Govern-
ment's goli reserve is to be shipped abroad
to purchase raw materials which will be used
in the manufacture of export articles. Un-
doubtedly the cotton textile supply and
demand council will have charge of allotting
a considerable portion of these funds and
it will exercise the necessary control over
the manufacture of the cotton and the ex-
portation of the finished textiles.

RECQRD NG 1ES: 1n despatch no. 2975, May 31, 1938, the mbassy
FILE -. C14.M
neluded certain corments on the policies of the new
aDG
minister of finance and of corunerce and industry, r.
Seihin Ikeda. Since that date some meager additions
to the abassy's information have been forthcoming in
amplification of that estimate, and are presented herein.
The manner in which Mr. Ikeda hopes to improve con-
ditions for business without compromising the major
economic objectives by wnich the Japanese Government
must be uided during the present hostilities has been
in part indicated by developments in the intervening
weeks. As suggested in the Embassy's telegram 336 of
May

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
May 28, 5 p.m., measures for the promotion of Japanese
exports constitute a fundamental problem for the new
minister. This problem, it appears, he proposes to
attack by tivin into the hanas of the various industries,
organized within themselves, a power over and responsi-
bility for certain control which was, beginning September
1937, enforced directly by the government. In this trend
I.r. Ikeda is working along lines natural to Japanese
business, in wnich guilas an: associations of various
kinds and of various deerees of autonomy have for cen-
turies played iaportant roles. Mr. Ikeda's long prac-
tical business experience has qualified him to recognize
the warrant for complaints from business against the in-
elasticity of administrative restrictions by covernment
orfices nastily orcanized since the connencement of hos-
tilities. The restrictions in the main are endorsed by
tne new minister; for the smooth enforcement of them he
believes the country's more or less autonomous organiza-
tions of industry and business can profitably be exploited.
The direction which r. Ikeda's thoucht is pursuing
in this matter is well set forth in a few sentences which
he included in a press interview on June 3, as translated
by the DolI news service: "hether the government will
establish a central organ for each industry, as is the
case in the Nazi system, is under study. I stand for
autonomous control as far as possible. Bureaus and sec-
tions of the commerce and industry ministry are too com-
plicated to meet the present wartime economic situation."
Meantime Mr. Ikeda aims also at alleviation of import
restrictions on essential raw materials, as an inducement
to

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
/. to expansion of export. He has announced it as his
desire so to link import control with the needs of the
export industries that raw materials for suoh industries
can be imported with no other limit than the volume of
export of product. He holds to the belief, it would
appear, that responsibility of an entire such industry
organized as an association is sufficient safeguard for
confining raw material purchases to a quantity which
will yield a product approximately proportioned to the
export demand existing at the time of completion of
manufacture.
Legislative provisions governing the "cooperative
councils" which are to act within the various industries
are set forth in enclosures to despatch no. 3032, June
21, 1938. As in other emergency legislation these pro-
visions are in general terms, and it is impossible, in
advance of observing how they are to be applied in prac-
tice, to judge whether Japanese industry is on the
threshold of being organized into units characteristic
of present-day Italy, or of present-day Germany,
thing different. whatever the actual change, it is
likely that Japan's economic structure will emerge from
the China conflict permanently altered in some of the
ways which we think of as fascist
Mr. Ikeda has thus far made only a beginning. That
he faces widespread business dissatisfaction with the
enforcement hitherto of import restrictions of raw
materials can be inferred from the insistent complaints
from industry. It is known that petitions have been
filed

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
filed for alleviation of the import restrictions on
rubber, electro-copper, stainless steel, leather, lac,
photographic gelatine, celluloid, sodium nitrate, anti-
mony, cobalt, mercury, cowhide, sugar, perfume, gum
Arabic, tin, coffee, cacao, lead, paraffin, zinc, wire,
wire rods, and printing ink.
There are other and less important aspects of Mr.
Ikeda's policy. He is attempting to reduce consumption
of goods by the people through widespread saving, and
a campaign to this end has been pressed with greater
intensity than by his predecessors. The automatic
though severe compulsion of reduced consumption which
comes with rapidly rising prices is a method which na-
tions engaged in armed conflict have almost universally
made use of, but Mr. Ikeda desires to exhaust every al-
ternative first. Another aspect of his policy is to
discourage exports to Manchuria and north China because
exports to regions included in the yen area make no con-
tribution to Japan's quest for foreign exchange in cur-
rencies unrelated to the yen. And great interest at-
taches to the solution he will have to offer to the
needs of economic development in north and central China,
where again his chief preoccupation appears to be to find
means of drawing on existing Japanese business experience
and organization instead of to build huge and completely
new structures (as seems to have been the trend in Man-
churia under the politico-economic monopoly ideas
promoted by the military).
But, as indicated above, the cardinal points of Mr.
Ikeda's policy so far revealed, are, first, the extending
of

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.97)
-5-
of as great autonomy as possible to organized councils
in the various industries in the administration of con-
trol, and, second, an effort to relieve the export
industries from the trials of raw material limitation
by linking permitted imports to volume of exports,
however great.
Respectfully yours,
S00-<1.
Joseph C. Grew.

official residence, and made representations eccording
to the precise statement appended hereto. My presenta-
tion was prepared in this form in order to convenience
the English interpreter, Mr. Tsuchiya, and to leave a
precise although informal record of what was said.
Mr. Tsuchiya held a copy of the document and rapidly
interpretcd each sentence as I read it to the Minister.
There was no interruption during the entire reading
and translation of this document.
The Minister after listening to my complete
representations said that he wished to express apprecia-
tion of the manner of my approach. His actual words as
translated by the interpreter were that the Minister
wished to express appreciation and gratitude for the
sympathy and friendliness* of my representations.
Frankly, the Minister's reaction somewhat surprised me
because I had spoken to him forcefully and emphatically
and had set forth in no uncertain terms the highly
unfavorable attitude of my Government and of the American
public towards Japan's conduct of the hostilities in
China

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
China, and I had rather expected a far less favorable
reception of my remarks. I think that the Minister's
reaction can only be expleined by the comments which I
advanced to him under the heading Japanese-American
Relations" and my observation under the heading General
Principles" that during the entire conflict of the past
year my Government has endeavored to follow a course
of strict impartielity and this attitude, we feel,
entitles us to express our views without risk of mis-
understanding.
The Minister then continued with the following
further comments. He would take a further opportunity,
he said, to discuss the points which I had raised.
These points required most careful investigation, con-
sideration and detailed examination but for the present
he wished to make the following observations:
1. The Japanese Government is making wholehearted
efforts to settle the conflict end to secure peace in
East Asia at the earliest possible moment. The Minister
hopes that we understand that desire.
2. The Japanese Government is giving most careful
thought to Japanese-American reletions. There must be
frank discussion and we must understand each other on
a basis of mutual fairness and justice.
3. The Open Door in China will be maintained. We
may rest assured that the Japanese Government will main-
tain full respect for the principle of equal opportunity.
In

=== Page 165 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
In some cases there may be temporary difficulties during
the period of hostilities not entirely satisfactory to
the American Government. These are purely temporary.
4. Indiscriminate bombing is strictly prohibited
by special instructions. In actuality a few Japanese
aviators have not had long enough training and miss
their marks which explains the damage of which I com-
plained. Every effort is to be made to avert this kind
of damage.
5. Full consideration is being given to the
return of American citizens to their goods and property.
The Minister hopes we will understand that ordinary
Japanese people are not allowed to follow the army but
only contractors and purveyors. He gave the illustra-
tion of sea gulls following a ship.
6. In Shanghai and Nanking there is now peace
and order but while the Japanese are pushing their drive
on Hankow those places are actually bases of military
activity. The Minister hopes that we will take that
fact into consideration.
Immediately at the commencement of the interview
and prior to my own representations General Ugaki said
that he desired to take up certain points with me and
he thereupon read in Japanese a document which was
interpreted into English by Mr. Tsuchiya. In reply to
my inquiry the Minister said that this document would
be sent me as soon as certain typographical corrections
had been made. The points which he raised were as follows:
Protection

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
Protection of Lives and Property of American Nationals
Desiring to prevent accidental injuries to American
nationals and their property in the area of hostilities
in China the Imperial Government has suggested to the
foreign ambassadors and ministers in Tokyo measures
for the protection of their nationals. It is also
stated in the above-mentioned note that in many cases
(actual examples listed below) the Chinese have taken
advantage of American property from a military stand-
point. The Japanese forces must of course attack
military objectives once they have been established by
the Chinese. In spite of these facts the Imperial Govern-
ment is giving the most careful thought and consideration
to harmonizing this situation with actualities. In our
note no. 972 we had informed the Japanese Government that
even if there are Chinese military establishments near
American property the American Government can exercise
no control over this and therefore the fact that such
military establishments exist in no way lessens the
responsibility of Japan for losses which American nationals
may sustain when such Chinese military establishments are
attacked by Japanese military forces. Although the
Japanese forces are endeavoring in every way to avoid
damage to American property it is very difficult in the
prosecution of hostilities to prevent inevitable loss of
some extent, especially in instances in which the Chinese
have clearly occupied Americen property. In view of this
1t

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
it is not at all logical that the responsibility should
be borne by Japan. As the American Government is aware
of the above realities the Japanese Government earnestly
hopes that we will wish to endeavor to prevent the
utilization of American property in a military sense by
the Chinese. If without making the above endeavor the
American Government speaks of responsibility on the part
of Japan alone, the feeling of partiality cannot be
removed.
I said to the Minister that I took note of his
statement and that I would deal with it in my subsequent
representations which could clearly indicate that most
if not all of the American property damaged by such
military operetions had been widely separated from any
Chinese military objective. Those representations clear-
ly indicated that the American Government must hold the
Japanese Government responsible for such losses and
damages.
List of Examples
1. At Shunteh, Hopeh Province, on November 12
of last year, the American Presbyterian church being
used as the staff headquarters of the Chinese army
was bombed.
2. At the time of the attack on Fenyang, Shansi
Province, on February 17 of this year the Chinese army
occupied military emplacements which placed an Ameri-
can church at their back and forced severe obstacles
on our forces in their selection of a method end an
opportunity for attack. Finally this church also
suffered bombardment.
3. There is information to the effect that on
May 14 of this year during the hostilities in the
neighborhood of Hsuchow, one part of the Chinese army
established a wireless set in an American church
building

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
building at Chunyin in Kiengsu Province and engaged
in communications.
4. On May 25 of this year Sen Chihti, head
of the Tsimo plain-clothes anti-Japanese group and
at the same time head of the military police, (said
to have 1500 subordinates) was discovered inside
Tsingtao. During the pursuit of this individual
he was hidden in the Tsungteh middle school under
the manegement of the Yanghsin Lu American Pres-
byterian church.
Articles in American Magazines disrespectful to Japan.
Recently there have appeared many articles dis-
respectful to the Japenese Imperial Household published
in American magazines (exemples listed below). Although
by taking suitable measures the Japanese authorities are
endecvoring to prevent exacerbation of the feelings of
the people towerds the United States, nevertheless if
these articles were made known to the Japenese people
the Minister fears that the incident concerning the
Imperial Household would give rise to a serious problem.
In view of the good relations between Japen and America
the continued appearance of such publications is a source
of concern to the Imperial Government. It would be
gratifying if the American Government would take suitable
measures in connection with this situation.
I said to the Minister that one of the fundamental
principles of the American Constitution and Government
was free speech and free press and that with the best
will in the world my Government could exert no control
over the press. When statements appeared in the American
press of a nature to injure the feelings of foreign
countries

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
countries, especially comments regarding foreign royalty,
the American Government deplored these utterances but it
had been shown by experience that when protests were
made they generally served only to draw greater public
attention to the utterances in question and often
resulted in throwing more fuel on the flames, whereas
if allowed to pass without comment such press utterances
generelly died & natural death very quickly and were
soon forgotten. It would therefore be highly inadvisable
for my Government to take steps along the lines of the
Minister's request. I added that our own Chief of State
and Government are continually subject to edverse
criticism, sometimes rather vitriolic criticism, by the
American press.

Disadvantages of American press announcements concerning

The Minister then said, with regard to the present
incident, the Japanese authorities in the field (Foreign
Office, army, navy) are all zealously working for a local
settlement

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)

settlement. However, the publication in the press by
the American Government of incidents which are progress-

of the American Government which not only does not con-
tribute towards a settlement of the case but on the
contrary, the Minister fears, delays the settlement.
With regard to local settlements, because of the manner
of treatment by the American Government as mentioned above,
the authorities in the field receive the impression that
the American Government believes that if it brings the
problem to the attention of the central authorities it
will be able to expedite the settlement. This has a
tendency to stiffen the attitude of the authorities in
the field. On the contrary when a matter is entrusted
to local settlement, if we cite the example of the
settlement of the recent question of the return of
American missionaries to Suchow, the rapidity of the
solution is clear. In this connection, in addition to
our full understanding of the foregoing circumstances
the Minister most earnestly hopes that for the solution
of the problems of the present hostilities the most
practical measures directly relative to actual conditions
will be adopted. Furthermore, on the part of the Japanese,
individuals from the concerned bureaus of the Foreign
Office, army and navy, are now being called to Tokyo and
the conditions minutely examined.
I promptly took issue with the Minister on his
point that we should deal exclusively with the authorities

=== Page 171 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
in the field rather than with the central authorities
and I pointed out that the Minister must realize that our
proper principal channel for explaining American in-
terests and for making representations if such interests
were injured was the Foreign Office in Tokyo, not the
army and navy, and that we must hold the Minister himself
directly responsible for dealing with the foreign rela-
tions of Japan.
(The Minister's meaning with regard to the publica-
tion by the American Government "of incidents which are
progressing towards a local settlement" was not entirely
clear but I suppose he had in mind various press releases
of the Department of State from time to time concerning
representations made to the Japanese Government and that
the knowledge of these representations had tended to
stiffen the attitude of the military and naval authorities
in the field. Considerable publicity was given in Japan
to an alleged statement by someone in the Department of
State that our representations concerning the return of
missionaries to Suchow had brought results with amazing
and almost unprecedented rapidity. It was of course
pure coincidence that the permits were given so soon
after our representations but the nature of the publicity
in Japan gave the impression that our Embassy possessed
great influence with the Foreign Office through which it
was able to get immediate results and this may well have
angered the military and naval authorities.)
Recent

=== Page 172 === (confidence 0.99)

Recent statement by the Japanese Spokesman in Shanghai
concernin: Extraterritoriality.
As a result of investigations made by the Imperial
Government of the reports which were circulated concern-
ing the statement by the spokesman of the Japanese Con-
sulate General in Shanghai concerning extraterritoriality,
it has been found that they were based upon a misunder-
standing. It goes without saying that the Japanese
Government has no intention of denying the right of
extraterritoriality in Chine which America and other
countries enjoy and of giving the present conditions in
China as an excuse. This is considered to be entirely
a problem between those various countries and China.
However, the Imperial Government strongly maintains
the view that it is only right, because of the present
hostilities between Japan and China extending over a
broad area, that the Japanese armed forces take whatever
measures are necessary from a military standpoint. This
is particularly justifiable when there is an attempt to
endanger the safety of the forces or when an act is done
or attempted which would injure the carrying out of
Japanese military activities. In such cases the army will
take necessary measures without questioning the national-
ity of the person. The above-mentioned statement by the
Japenese spokesman in Shanghai was, after all, nothing
more than an expression to the above purport. It may be
said that the statement in question was entirely separate
from the question of extraterritoriality. The Imperial
Government sincerely desires to correct the misunderstanding

on the part of the American Government and to obtain its
understanding of the true motive or the Imperial Govern-

Conversation with His Excellency General. Ugaki,
Minister for Foreign Affairs, July 4, 1938.
I have not wished to bother His Excellency by
frequent visits until he had had an opportunity to
become settled in office.
I have therefore taken up either with the Vice
Minister or the Director of the American Bureau the
various issues and cases that have arisen.
Furthermore, I feel that the Minister has been
making efforts on his own initiative to find solutions
to various difficulties for American interests in China
arising out of the present conflict.
I have wished to watch the workin: out of those
efforts which I have reported to my Government and
which are fully appreciated.
Now, however, I feel that the time has come for a
talk along general lines.
I hope that the Minister will regard our talk
today as concentrating what might have been said in
several interviews since he assumed office.
I do not wish to present any diplomatic document,
formal or informal. My representations will be oral.
But if it will convenience the Minister in recording
our conversation, I shall be happy to leave with him
these rough notes, not as a diplomatic document but
merely as an informal guide to what has been orally
presented.

RESPECT FOR AMERICAN PROPERTY RIGHTS
At my first interview with the Minister when I
asked what report I might make to Washington concerning
the Minister's attitude towards American interests in
China, I was very much gratified when His Excellency
replied that he would guarantee the protection of those
interests.
My Government has been informed of those assurances.
I now feel that the Minister would wish to be
informed of the great and widespread injury to.American
property in China at the hands of Japanese military and
naval forces during the year since the hostilities in
China began.
A list has therefore been prepared, which I shall
leave with His lxcellency, giving an accurate and item-
ized statement of some of these damages.
This list is however only partial and not complete.
The Minister will note that much of the damage
has occurred through the indiscriminate bombing of
buildings carefully und conspicuously marked with
American flags.
This includes many hospitals, missions, schools,
churches and colleges.
We believe that very few of these damaged buildings
were situated nearby to any Chinese military objectives.
As a typical case, one of very many cases, I shall
ask the Interpreter to be good enough to translate to
His Excellency a recent letter from our Embassy in Peiping
to Mr. Horiuchi, Counselor of the Japanese Embassy in
that city. (appended)
Not only has American property been widely injured
or wholly destroyed but American lives have been con-
tinually placed in immediate danger.
These incidents obviously have created a most
unfortunate reaction in the United States, not only in
the interested business circles and among the many
millions of Christians whose churches, missions, schools
and hospitals have been damaged or destroyed, but on
American public opinion as a whole.
The

=== Page 176 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
The deplorable effect on Japanese-American rela-
tions is obvious.
To overcome this effect and to carry out the
guarantees which the Minister was good enough to give
me in our conversation of May 3l, I urge His Excellency,
under the heading of respect for American property
rights, to take concrete steps as follows:
(a) to ensure the exercise of particular care by
the armed forces of Japan in China to avoid damaging
American properties by direct military action, especially
through bombing activities from airplanes;
(b) to ensure the restoration to American citizens
of the full possession and unhampered use of their
properties in the areas controlled by the Japanese wherein
warfare has ceased;
(c) and to ensure compensation for such loss and
damage as have been caused by Japanese military operations
in China.
MAINTENANCE OF THE PERSONAL RIGHTS OF AMERICAN CITIZENS
Under this general heading, several important
desiderata come into view.
First and foremost, of course, is the prime im-
portance of avoiding danger to American lives by
indiscriminate bombing operations.
The charge that many of these operations in the past
have been indiscriminate can hardly be denied, for the
results have been clear.
The Americn buildings were clearly marked by
American flags; many or most of them were widely separate
from any Chinese military objectives.
Yet they were bombed nevertheless, and American
lives were thereby placed in jeopardy.
Secondly, American citizens have been in various
cases and localities molested or affronted by Japanese
soldiers.
There

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
There have been, among other cases, the Allison
incident and the Thomson incident in Nanking, and the
Massie incident in Tsingtao, all cases of personal
assault without good cause. The slapping across the
face of American citizens, including the wife of an
American naval officer, by Japanese soldiers, cannot
pass unnoticed in my country. Were the positions
reversed, I cannot believe that the Japanese Government
would be inclined to tolerate such incidents.
Of an even graver nature was the Scovel incident
in Tsining where an American missionary was shot by a
drunken Japanese soldier.
I shall not now go into these cases in detail
because they have been or are being dealt with in-
dividually, and today I wish to speak along more
general lines.
But my Government feels very strongly that greater
control and care should be exercised by the Japanese
armed forces in avoiding injury and indignities to
American citizens.
Finally, under this heading, I wish to speak of
the rights of American citizens to visit and control
their goods and properties and to resume their lawful
occupations in the areas controlled by Japan wherein
hostilities have terminated.
I know that the Minister has been making efforts
along those lines and, in the case of Nanking, that
passes are beins issued in certain cases.
But in a locality where hundreds of Japanese
civilians have already established themselves, we feel
that the bars should be let down more generally and
that American citizens, having lawful interests and
occupations in Nanking and other localities in the
occupied areas where warfare has ceased should be
permitted to proceed forthwith.
The continued occupation by Japanese troops of the
University of Shanghai, American property, is particular-
ly to be deplored. I may say that there are some millions
of Baptists in the United States keenly interested in
that question today.
In

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
In this general connection there is a long out-
standing case of the dctention and partial destruction
at Nanking of a cargo of wood oil belonging to an
American concern, the Werner G. Smith Company, which
the owners wish to ship to Shanghai, yet permission has
been withheld for many months.
All of thee situations and incidents have created
an unfortunate impression on my Government and on American
public opinion.
The smoothing out of these cases is essential in
the interests of good relations between our countries.
Summarizing the desiderata under this heading, I
would mention the following points:
(a) avoidance of danger to American lives through
indiscriminate bombing operations and other acts;
(b) avoidance of assaults and indignities inflicted
on American citizens by Japanese armed forces;
(c) restitution of the rights of American citizens
to visit and control their goods and properties and to
take up their lawful occupations in the areas controlled
by Japan in which hostilities have ceased.
MAINTENANCE OF EQUALITY OF OPPORTUNITY IN JAPANESE
CONTROLLED ARLAS IN CHINA AS BETWEEN JAPANESE AND OTHERS
Under this heading I refer to the avoidance of
restrictions and obstacles to American trade and other
enterprise as might result from the setting up of
"special companies", officially supported and granted
preferred status;
Avoidance of the granting of monopolies;
And avoidance of the establishment of exchange control
involving restrictions upon the trade between the United
States and China while at the same. time allowing the free
movement of funds and goods between Japan and China.
In

=== Page 179 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
In that respect the situation in Manchuria, where
American enterprise and trude are subject to restrictions
in favor of Japanese enterprise and trade, and in marked

assured in theory but denied in practise, is illustrative
of what we do not desire to see occur elsewhere in China.
This is a point upon which I would particularly
like to be able to communicate to my Government assurances

PROTECTION OF LEGITIMAT AMERICAN FINANCIAL INTERESTS
Included under this heading of desiderata are the
