GINTRAL PRINCIPLES
but while the foregoing desid rata are of great
importance, I fec! it my duty to convey to the Minister
certain intersts end concerns of the American Government
end people along broader lines.
These interests and concerns have from time to time
been publicly expressed by Mr. Hull with admirable clarity.
I refer in this connection to Mr. Hull's public
statements of July 16 and August 23, 1937, making clear
and applicable to the Pacific area the principles by
which the Americon Government is being guided, principles
with which Japan is in direct conflict.
I furthermore refer to Mr. Hull's speeches before
the National Press Club in Vashington on March 17 and

No doubt the Minister has seen these various pub-
lished statements and speeches; if not, they must be in
the files of the Gaimusho.
These statements and speeches by the American
Secretary of State have, among many other clearly stated
facts, indicated that the existence and circumstances
of

=== Page 184 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
of the present conflict in China is deplored by the
American people who h ve become increasingly perturbed
by the methods employed by Japen to resolve its dis-
putes with China.
During the entire conflict of the past year my
Government has endeavored to follow a course of strfot
impartielity.
This ettitude, we feel, entitles us to express our
views without risk of misunderstending.
In this world of ours no nation and no people can
escape the effects of warfare enyvhere.
The present manifestation of Japan's foreign policy
and the methods which the Japanese armed forces are em-
ploying in pursuit of that policy are looked upon with
deep regret by the Americen Government and people.
Not only on grounds of humanity but also on grounds
of the menace to Aaerican life and property, the vide-
spread bombing of civilian populetions in China has pro-
foundly shocked both our Governrent and people.
There may be as has been claimed sanction under the
rules of war for bombing defended areas, but it is my
profound conviction that conditions have altered since
the rules of war were formulated - in aays which preceded
the invention of airplanes - and that the conscience of
mankind abhors the wholesale destruction from the air
of innocent non-combatants.
My Government is most deeply anxious that the conflict
be concluded and that peace be restored at the earliest
possible moment, with due regard for the establishment and
maintenance of orderly processes in the relations of nations,
elong lines consistent with the provisions of existing
international commitments and with principles of justice
and equity with regard to all concerned.
These concerns of my Government and the American
people are broad and fundamental.
I cannot too earnestly stress the public thought
and comment that are being give to these matters in
my country today, where acts and facts speak louder
than words.
Now

=== Page 185 === (confidence 1.00)
-12-
Now I have spoken vith the utmost frankness, but
in all friendliness and friendship.
I shall say nothing of this interview to the press.
Perhaps the Minister may wish to tell the press merely
that I came to discuss Japanese American relations in
general terms.
Once agein I earnestly appeal for His Excellency's
cooperation and most creful thought in considering the
desiderate and facts presented.

Peiping, June 21, 1938.
My dear Mr. Horiuchi:
I wish to inform you that the American Southern
Baptist Mission, an American organization, reports
that on the morning of June 15, 1938, with clear
visibility and no wind, tvo Japanese airplanes bombed
its premises at Pingtu, Shantung; that two bombs were
dropped over the mission's Boys' School; that both
bombs exploded, one striking 15 feet and the other
25 feet from the residence of the Americen principal
cf the school; that extensive damage was done to the
principal's residence and the school buildings; and
thet the Boys' School copound is about one-eighth of
a mile south of the city wall and not close to any
military objective. The mission states that, after
circling, the planes returned over the same course and
dropped tvo bombs about 50 feet west of the mission's
Girls' School compound which is close to the city wall;
and that the concussion caused damage to bildings
on this compound and adjacent mission property. The
mission reports that six American flags were flying
on the compound at the time of the bombing, and it adds
that, during the occupation of Pingtu this year by
Tateki Horiuchi, Esquire,
Counselor of Embassy,
Embassy of Japan,
Peiping.

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
forces of the Japanese Army, various compounds of the
mission were visited by officers in command of detach-
ments. The mission states that Japanese bombing planes
have flown low over its compounds on several occasions.
This information has been communicated to the
American Embassy at Tokyo for transmission to the
Japanese euthorities there.
Sincerely yours,
(Signed) Laurence E. Salisbury

Translation by the Embassy at Tokyo of the notes
of the Foreign Minister for his interview with the
Ambassador on July 4th. These notes were received
from the Foreign Minister's private secretary on
July 5, 1938.
M.W.S.:r
STRICTLY CONFIDENTIAL
July 2, 1938 - American I.
Interview between the Foreign Minister and
Ambassedor Grew, July 4, 1938.
Reference Material.
1.
QUESTION OF THE PROTECTION OF THE LIVES AND
PROPERTY OF AMERICAN NATIONALS.
The Imperial Government, because of its intention to
prevent, to as great an extent as possible, accidental
injuries resulting fror engagements between the Japanese
and Chinese forces to American nationals and their
property in the area of hostilities, has suggested to
the foreign Ambassadors and Ministers in Tokyo measures
for the protection of the nationals of third countries,
such as was recently the case in regard to the broadening
of the area of hostilities. As was also stated in the
above-mentioned official letter, there are many examples
(actual examples are given separately) of the Chinese
taking advantage, from a military standpoint, of the
property

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
property of American nationals. As you must realize,
it is abeolutely necessary, in view of the existence
of hostilities between Japen and China, that the
Japenese forces attack military emplacements once they
have been established by the Chinese.
While harmonizing its earnest hope for the protection
of American nationals and their property with the neces-
sities of actual conditions under which the Imperial
armed forces can not but attack the property of American
nationals which is utilized to military advantage by
the Chinese forces, the Imperial Government is continuing
to give the most careful thought and consideration to
the proper measurs to be taken in such instances.
However, Your Excellency has steted the view that,
even if there are military establishments placed near
the property of American nationals by the Chinese forces,
the American Government can exercise no control over
this, and accordingly, the fact that such military estab-
lishments exist in no way lessens the responsibility of
Japan for losses which American nationals may sustain
when such Chinese military establishments are attacked
by the Japanese forces (The American Ambassador's note
No. 972).
Although the Imperial armed forces are endeavoring
on their part, by exhausting every available means, not
to cause damage to the property of American nationals in
the attack on military emplscements of the Chinese armed
forces near the property of American nationals, it is
most difficult in the prosecution of hostilities to
prevent

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
prevent unavoidable loss of sone extent. This loss
is unavoidavle in the same manner as is that loss to
American nationals arisin from the hostilities between
Japan and China in instances in which the Chinese have
clearly occupied property of Americen nationals. In
view of this, it is not at ell logical that responsibility
should be borne by Japan.
The Imperial Government cannot but earnestly hope
that Your xccllency's Governnent, being cognizant of
the above realities, will want to endeavor to prevent th e
utilization in a military sense by the Chinese of the
property of American nationals. If Your Execllency's
Government, without making the above endeavor, merely
speaks of responsibility on the nort of Japen alone,
the feeling of partiality cannot be removed.
2.
THL QUESTION OF LISR. SPECTFUL ARITICLES IN
AMERICAN MAGAZINES.
Recently there have been many articles disrespectful
to the Japanese Imp rial Household published in American
magazines. (Actual examples listed separately).
Although, by taking suitable measures, we are en-
deavoring to prevent exacerbation of the feeling of our
people towards America, if the articles were made known
to our people, I am afraid that the incident concerning
the Imperial Household would give rise to a serious prob-
lem. In view of the good relations between Japan and
America, the continued appearance of such publications
is a source of concern to the Imperial Government. It
would be gratifying if suitable measures were taken upon
the

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
the consideration of the above situation by Your Ex-
cellency's Government.
3.
THE QUESTION OF THI LILTHOD OF HANDLING THE
DAMAG. S TO PROPLRTY OF AM RICAN NATIONALS.
In relation to the present incident the Japenese
authorities in the field (Foreign Office, Army, Navy)
are all zealously working for a locel settlement.
However, the publication in the newspepers, et cetera,
of Your Excellency's country, of incidents which are
progrossing towards a local sett ement is a measure on
the part of Your Excelleney's Government which not only
does not contribute towards s settlement of the se,
but also, I am afraid, on the contrary delays the settle-
nent.
In regard to loenl settlements, because of the
manner of troatment by Your Excellency's Government, as
mentioned above, the authorities in the field, receiving
the impression that "the American Government believes
that if it brings the problem to the attention of the
Central authorities it will be able to expedite the
settlement", have a tendency to stiffen their attitude.
On the contrary, vhen a matter is entrusted to local
settlement, if we cite the example of the settlement of
the recent question of the return of American missionaries
to Suchow, the rapidity of the solution is clear. In this
connection, in addition to your full understanding of the
above circumstances, I must most earnestly hope that for
the

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.98)
-5-
the solution of the problems of this incident, the most
practical measures directly relative to actual conditions
will be adopted. rurther, on our part, individuals from
the bureaus concerned or tne roreign Office, Army and
C
now
Navy are/being called to Tokyo and the conditions minutely
examined.
4.
THE INCIDENT RELATING TO THE STATEMENT OF THE
ABOLISHMENT OF ATRATARRITORLALITY IN THE
OCCUPIED ARLAS.
As a result of the investigations made by the
Imporial Government of the reports which were circulated
coicerning the statement by the spokesman of the Japanese
Consulate General in Shanghai, it has been determined
that they were based upon a misunderstanding.
It goes without saying that the Imperial Government
has no intention of denying the right of extraterritorial-
ity in China wnich America and other countries have, and
giving as a reason the present conditions in China.
This is considered to be entirely a problem cetween
those various countries and China. However, the Imperial
Government strongly maintains the view that it is only
right, because of the present hostilities between Japan
and China extending over a broad area, that the Japanese
armed rorces take whatever measures are necessary from a
military standpoint. It is particularly justifiable when
measures are necessary for the safety of armed roroes,
that is, when there is an attempt to endanger the safety
of the forces, or an act is done or attempted which would
injure

=== Page 216 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
injure the carrying out of our armed forces' military
activities. In such an instance the army will take
necessary measures without considering the nationality
of the person.
The above-mentioned statement by the spokesman of the
Japanese Consulate General in Shanghai was, after all,
nothing mo tnan an expr@ssion to the apove purport.
In sunmary it may be statea tnat tnis item is
separate rrom tne question of extraterritoriality.
The Imperial Government sincerely desires to correct
the misunderstanding on the part of the American Govern-
ment and to optain its understanding of the true motive
or tne Imperial Government.
SEVERAL EXAMPLES OF INSTANCES IN WHICH
PROSECUTION OF HOSTTLITLS ON UUR PART HAS
DEEN HIND RED BY THE UTTLIZATLON OF AMERICAN
CHURCHES BY THE CHINESE.
1. At Shunteh, Hopeh I'rovince, on November 12 of
last year, the Americen Presbyterian church being used
as the staff headquarters of the Chinese army was bombed.
2. At the time of the atteck on Fenyang, Shansi
Province, on February 17 of this year the Chinese army
occupied military emplecements which placed an American
church at their back and forced severe obstacles on our
forces in their selection of a method and an opportunity
for attack. Finally this church also suffered our
bombardment.
3. There is information to the effect that on May 14
of

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
of this year during the hostilities in the neighborhood
of Hsuchow, one part of the Chinese army established
a wircless set in an American church building at Chunyin
in kiangsu Province and engaged in communications.
4. On May 25 of this year Sen chihti, nead of the
Tsimo plain-clothes anti-Japanese group and at the same
time head of the military police, (said to have 1500
subordinates) was discovered inside Tsingtao. During
the pursuit of this individual he was hidden in the
Tsungteh middle school under the management of the
Yanghsin Lu American Presbyterian church.

published disrespectful articles:

With reference to my telegram no. 437, July 4,
14.00
7 p.m., I have the honor to inform the Department that
F/WB
in the course of my conversation with the Minister for
601
Foreign Affairs on that date, General Ugaki made the
following comments, in translation, concerning the
protection of lives and property of American nationals
in China:
The

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
The Imperial Government, because of its intention to
prevent, to as great an extent as possible, accidental
injuries resulting from engagements between the Japanese
and Chinese forces to American nationals and their
property in the area of hostilities, has suggested to
the foreign Ambassadors and Ministers in Tokyo measures
for the protection of the nationals of third countries,
such as was recently the case in regard to the broadening
of the area of hostilities. As was also stated in the
above-mentioned official letter, there are many examples
(actual examples are given separately) of the Chinese
taking advantage, from a military standpoint, of the
property of American nationals. As you must realize,
it is absolutely necessary, in view of the existence
of hostilities betveen Japan and China, that the
Japanese forces attack military emplacements once they
have been established by the Chinese.
While harmonizing its earnest hope for the protection
of American nationals and their property with the noces-
sities of actual conditions under which the Imperial
armed forces cannot but attack the property of American
nationals which isutilized to military advantage by
the Chinese forces, the Imperial Government is continuing
to give the most careful thought and consideration to
the proper measures to be taken in such instances.
"However, Your Excellency has stated the view that,
even if there are military establishments placed near
the

=== Page 223 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
the property of American nationals by the Chinese forces,
the American Government can exercise no control over
this, and accordingly, the fact that such military estab-
lishments exist in no way lessens the responsibility of
Japan for losses which American nationals may sustain
when such Chinese military establishments are attacked
by the Japanese forces (the American Ambassador's note
No. 972).
"Although the Imperial armed forces are endeavoring
on their part, by exhausting every available means, not
to cause damae to the property of American nationals in
the attack on military emplacements of the Chinese armed
forces near the property of American nationals,it is
most difficult in the prosecution of hostilities to
prevent unavoidable lcss of some extent. This loss
is unavoidable in the same manner as is that loss to
American nationals arising from the hostilities between
Japan and China in instances in which the Chinese have
clearly occupied property of American nationals. In
view of this, it is not at all logical that responsibility
should be borne by Japan.
"The Imperial Government cannot but earnestly hope
that Your Excellency's Government, being cognizant of
the above realities, will want to endeavor to prevent the
utilization in a military sense by the Chinese of the
property of American nationals. Ir Your Excellency's
Government, without making the above endeavor, merely
speaks of responsibility on the part of Japan alone,
the feeling of partiality cannot be removed.
Several

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.98)
. i.
-4-
"SEVERAL EXAMPLES OF INSTANCES IN WHICH PROSECUTION
OF HOSTILITIES ON OUR PART HAS BEEN HINDERED BY
THE UTILIZATION OF AMERICAN CHURCHES BY THE CHINESE
"1. At Shunteh, Hopeh Province, on November 12 of
last year, the American Presbyterian church being used
as the staff headquarters of the Chinese army was bombed.
"2. At the time of the attack on Fenyang, Shansi
Province, on February 17 of this year the Chinese army
occupied military emplacements which placed an American
church at their back and forced severe obstacles on our
forces in their selection of a method and an opportunity
for attack. Finally this church also suffered our
bombardment.
"3. There is information to the effect that on May 14
of this year during the hostilities in the neighborhood
of Hsuchov, one part of the Chinese army established
a wireless set in an American church building at Chunyin
in Kiangsu Province and engaged in communicetions.
"4. On May 25 of this year Sen Chihti, head of the
Tsimo plein-clothes anti-Jopenese group end at the same
time head of the military police, (seid to have 1500
subordinates) was discovered inside Tsingtao. During
the pursuit of this individual he was hidden in the
Tsungteh middle school under the management of the
Yanghsin Lu American Presbyterian church."
I said to the Minister that I took note of his
statement and that I would deal with it in my subsequent
representations

representations which could cleerly indicate that most
if not all of the American property damaged by such

Chinese military objective. Those representations clear-
ly indicated that the American Government must hold the
Japanese Government responsible for such losses and
damages.
Respectfully yours,
.
Joseph C. Crev.

A Domei report from Warsaw reported on July l9th

The occurrence of this inoident at the prssent time
has plaoed the Japanese Govermment in a dilomma. If the
Russian troops fail to withdraw in complianoe with Japan-
ese demands, the Japanese Covernment is faced with the
absolute neoessity of driving them back by foree, and
this decision is reported to have been reached by the
authorities conoerned, though some hope is apparently
still held that the withdrawal will be acsomplished
peacefully. Failing to use foree to dislodge the Rus-
sians would cause the impressien to cireulate both in
Manchukuo and in China that Japan is unable to cope
with the situation. If, on the other hand, foree is
resorted to b the Japanese, there is the risk of be-
coming involved in a war with Russia while still engaged
in hostilities in China. Infommed Japanese appear to
believe that this risk can be safely taken, as they
think that the result of the wee of foroe would be to
foree the kussian authorities to relinquish their stand,
and in support of this belief is given the Amur River
incident of last summer. The fallacy in this opinion
seams to be that at thet time Japan was free to ongage

=== Page 255 === (confidence 0.99)

seem fairly clear. If the impending China Board (see the

with and interests in China, a Foreign Minister and his staff
could easily see that not only would the Foreign Office be out
out of much of its work but also its relations with foreign
countries would be embarrassingly complicated by its impotence
in the many issues having to do with China. General Ugaki

subordinated to the Foreign Office -- whether only in matters
touching foreign relations or in all its functions is not clear.
In the five-minister conference he found himself alone in

Finance) and his resignation was the consequence.
If General Ugaki desired, by submitting his resignation for
consideration, to force a decision between the Foreign Office
and the military, the sten was ill conceived. The proposed China
Board does not a pear to the Jtpanese public as a stepping forth
by the military into wider political fields; and the condemnatory
tone of the press toward General Ugaki's action was adequate proof
that there is no widespread apprehension that expansion of the
power of the five-minister conferences (for such in effect would
be the result of establisning the Cnina oard) will jeopardize the
nation's interests. If General Ugaki wished to focus attention on
an issue between the military and others, he failed; the publio
does not view extension of the powers of the five-minister conferenoe
as a concession to the military, but sees it simply as a practical
expedient for concentrating the channels of unified control,
necessary because of the hostilities. .

THE FOREIGN SERVICE

AMERICAN EMBASSY

Tokyo, 0ctober 4, 1938.
SIATE
SUBJECT:
THE C TEA BOARD (TAI SHI IN).
57
(1)
21 M1
150 820
B
844.00
Depariment of Srate
not
Fn
844.002
894.00/819
793.94
In U.S A.
ASSISTANT SECRETARY
Thdjjonorable
ARTMENT
The Jecretary of State
CF STATE
A-M
Jashington.
ar
The mbassy has had occasion at various times during
rectht ears to invite the Department's attention to a groping
in Japan toward new and more centralized policy-determining
bodies. This trend has paralleled the concurrent movement
FILED
away from party politics (in even the attenuated form previously
existin, in Japan) and away from the ordinarily-organized
Cabinet as the chief instrument of sovernment. The trend was
observable in the establishment of Cabinet counselors, later
in the powers placed in Imperial Headcuarters, and even more
F/FG
recently in the importance of the five-minister conferences.
The

=== Page 258 === (confidence 0.99)
5277
-2-
The present manifestation of the trend is the impending
establishment of the Tai Shi In (China Board, or instituto
with regard to China), to be an organ in which to conoentrato
direction of Japan's relations with and interests in China.
Prediction has been made in the press that the China
Board will be established and commence functioning by the
close of October. There has been no authoritative statement
of what vill be its form or powers, but announcement has been
made that about October 8 promulgation of an ordinance with
regard to it is likely. It is feared that such promulgation
will be too late for inclusion of translation in the pouch
by which this despatch is coing forward (leaving yokohama
October 8).
There has nevertheless been some credible discussion in
the newspapers of the probable provisions of the forthcoming
ordinance. The recent resignation of Foreign Minister Ugaki
because of his opposition to the provisions being considered
by the Cabinet removes the only obstacle in view, and it now
appears even more likely than before that such provisions as
are being considered will be put into effect. The newspaper
discussion therefore seems of probable dependability.
Accoraing to the press, the China Board will operate
entirely within the structure of the Cabinet. Its director
will be the Premier; it will have four vice directors in the
persons of the War Minister, the Navy Minister, the Foreign
Minister, and the Minister of Finance and of Commerce and
Industry. These are the five individuals who have made up
the five-minister conferences, that system of war cabinet within
the

=== Page 259 === (confidence 0.99)
6278
-3-
the Cabinet which in the last months has become more and
more dominant in the country's governing.
Under its director and four vice direotors the China
Board will have, according to the press, a large teohnical
and administrative staff, defined in the usual Japanese
manner by court rank. There will be three bureaus: general
affairs, economics, ana cultural relations. The chief of
each bureau is to be of cholkunin rank. Within the China Board
there will be a special deliberative body made up of expert
advisers and representatives of important elements of Japanese
public opinion. The Board will also have a "contact" committee
with some sort of intermediary capacity between the China Board
organization on the one hand and on the other hand the five-
minister conierences.
Again acccrding to the press, the China Board's funotion
is not only the direction of policy but also the exercise
of actual administrative control in the field. Branches will
be established in the occupied areas of China at a number of
points: Peiping, algan, and Shanghai have been mentioned. The
press anticipates that the oard is to function only for the
duration of the hostilities.
It would apoear that the conferences of the five ministers
are to continue to be the centralized policy-determining
authority for problems and interests in China, and that the
new China Board is an effort to create an organization to make
more effective the control exercised by the five-minister
conferences. The development is in part an empirical consequence
of

=== Page 260 === (confidence 0.98)
6279
-4-
of the relative success of those conferences in the last
months; it is a crystalization of a process of conoentrating
of authority which has been goin forward with the hostilities.
But at tis point it is necessary to temper the observation
that the China 3oard is another step in the direction of
concentration of power by again observing that it is expected
to be a body within the structure of the Cabinet. Japanese
leaders who foresee den er in possible assumption of exolusive
political control by the military apnear to derive satisfaction
from the fact that the movement to establish the new body has
been guided into the direction of subordination to the Cabinet
rather tian, for example, into the direction of subordination
Fuminiaro
to Imperial  eadquarters. It is probable that Premier Konog
sees as his most responsible task, at this juncture of Japah's
history, the prevention of complete political control of the
country by the military. This is not to assert that Prince
Konoe is antagonistic to the objectives of the military class --
on the contrary, there is aaple evidence thet he shares the
same strongly nationalistic views and hieh belief in an expanding
national destiny; but at the same time candid observation of his
methods leaves room for doubt that he sees hope in abandoning
the nation to leadership by the military themselves. It would
seem to be a safe assumption that Prince Konoe considers the
China Soard a triumph of his efforts at moderating the pace of
political change in Jupan.
If the establislment of the China Board leads to better
enforcement in the field of instructions issuing from Tokyo,
it is not all loss from the point of view of the interests of the
United States. The Department needs no reminding of the many
instances

=== Page 261 === (confidence 0.98)
6280
-5-
instances in which gratifying assurances accorded our
representations in Tokyo have failed of execoution by the
Japanese authorities in China. It is undoubtedly part of
the hope back of the establishment of the China Board that
it will mean stricter coordination. In my conversation with
the Premier on October 3 (teleeram 640, October 3, 4 p.m.)
he spoke specifically of the China Board as being aimed at
increasing direct control by the authorities in Tokyo over
the Japanese authorities in China.
But even the most hopeful interpretation of this newest
development can not conceal the great dangers which inhere
in it for the future. China is overwhelmingly Japan's most
important problem. It is not difficult to foresee a sweeping
effect on other already-operatin branches of the Japanese
government by reason of the establishing of a new governmental
body, hi hly led, appropriating to itself all matters having
to do with China. It is furthermore a matter of apprehension
that this change may survive as a workaile instrument of
centralized control, but may lose its present dominantly civilian
direction.

Mr. Hornbeck
Reference Tokyo's 3380 of November 1,
1933, entitled "National Mobilization".
Transmitted with this despatch is a
comprehensive menorandum preoared by
Mr. Max W. Schmidt, Third Secretary of
Embassy, entitled "Mobilization in Japan".
While most of the material in the
memorandun is familiar to the De artment
it is gathered together here for the first
time in a form convenient as a basis for
further study.
The memorandum traces in an able
manner the historical evolution of the
so-called "National General Mobilization"
movement from the early Munitions Indus-
try Mobilization Law through the Cabinet
Deliberative Body and Cabinet Investiga-
tion Bureau of 1935 into the more recent
Cabinet Planning Board created in October
1937 and down to the National General
Mobilization Law of the present day.
Coincidental with the evolution of these
laws and organs has been the growth of a
political doctrine akin to totalitarianism
culminating

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
culminating in the demand for a single
party system of government. Considerable
discussion is devoted to the aims of the
National General Mobilization Law and an
outline is provided of the more important
administrative organs which have been
recently instituted. The writer's main
theme is that mobilization in Japan is
only a part of the present tendency toward
Japanese totalitarianism. As to the
evolution of the present organs of govern-
ment he says that a single party may de-
velop but a dictator is hardly expected to
appear. "The Government" will control but
the Diet will probably be convened in ful-
fillment of the letter of the Constitution.
The writer sees in the National General
Mobilization Law not merely a program to
facilitate the conduct of hostilities but
also an attempt to place Japan in a posi-
tion to dominate and exploit the Far East.
With regard to the effect of "National
General Mobilization" upon the people, the
writer appears to feel that there is in-
creasing unemployment and hardships but no
threat of rebellion or resistance to the
national policy.
Comment:
It wouli seem to be well not to lose
sight of the fact in connection with the
movenent

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-3-
movement described in the memorandum that
many of the conditions which impel peoples
towards what is known as a "totalitarian"
form of government are lacking among the
Japanese. Moreover, it is not improbable
that the Japanese will, at the conclusion
of the present hostilities, reject many of
the restrictions that have been placed on
their economic and political life and re-
vert as they have often done in the past to
an oligarchical form of government peculiar
to them.
I think you will wish to read in full
page 3 of the Embassy's covering despatch
which gives the essential conclusions of
the memorandum and comments adequately on
their importance.

Sir:
1/
I nave the honor to enclose a memorandum entitled
"Mobilization in Japan", pre ared by Mr. Max W. Schmidt,
Third Secretary of the Embassy.
The memorandum deals with a movement now in process
the importance of which is daily becoming more manifest.
The word mobilization has been used in relation to this
movement only for lack of a term which would be concisely
descriptive

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
descriptive of conscious effort, under the control of a oentral
directorate, to rationalize and integrate all aspeots of na-
tional activity. All nations, including even those which
firmly rest on democratic principles, are today alive to the
impossibility of conducting a major war without coordination
of national r sources, and as brought out in the memorandum,
practically every nation has today some plan for coordinating
its industrial, economic and financial resources to be put
into effect in the event of war. The plan now being put into
effect by Japan, however, is not intended to be merely for
the duration of the hostilities with China: it is being in-
stituted at this time for the reason that Japan, perhaps more
than any other important Power, would be unable to conduct a
major war without arastic coordination and control of resouroes;
but the trend toward national mobilization had set in before
the hostilities began. The memorandum makes entirely clear
the ultimate objectives of those promoting and developing this
movement, and if they are to have their way - and no competent
observer will hazard the definite prediction that they will
not - a change out of all recognition of the economic and in-
dustrial systems may be expected. Indications of an altera-
tion in fact in the political structure are also present:
the political parties are completely impotent and there is
much talk - and much confusion - about the setting up of a
national party.
These far-reaching changes have naturally given rise
to the definite conclusion, especially abroad, that Japan
is evolving into a totalitarian state. There is nowadays
a

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.98)
6289
-3-
a too-common habit of placing political movements into
carefully divided categories and of attaching to each
movement some familiar and currently used designation.
As drastic as are the changes to be carried out under
national mobilization, we do not believe that there will
emerge a state which in its superficial aspects and in
many of its functional aspects, will be on all fours with
Germany and Italy. It is unlikely that the national polity
will be altered or that there will be material revision of
the organic laws of the several branohes of the Governmont.
Nor do we think it likely that a personal dictatorship
would be tolerated. We envisage rather a retention and
strengthening of the Emperor cult, and unification of the
various elements which now rule the country, perhaps even-
tually taking form in an organization by which the masses and
all forms of national activity will be regimented, coordinated
and integrated. What is evolving is totalitarianism, if one
insists on using that term, but it will be totalitarianism
sui generis.
I have complimented r. Schmidt on this memorandum,
Which is based largely on source material, there being avail-
able nothing on the subject in any foreign language and sur-
prisingly little by way of interpretation and commentary in
Japanese. By dint of application and industry Mr. Schmidt
2
has laid down the basis of further and continued study of an
important movement the end of which is not in view.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.99)
MOBILIZATION IN JAPAN
Historicel Development.
It is a well recognized fact that war as it is con-
ducted today vitally involves each individual of the various
nations engaged. The current cost of war and the expenditure
and loss of national wealth are so great that no nation can
affcrd to embark upon major hostilities without first es-
tablishing and effecting, to as great an extent as possible,
a program (as the Japanese term it) for "national general
mobilization". In fact the mobilization of an army, or armies,
is only a minor part of the mobilization of a nation's economy,
society and morale and of the coordination ana direction of
its every strength and resource.
Japan, unfortunately for that country, has had to pioneer
in the field of this modern mob:lization while at the same
time carrying on extensive military operations at a consid-
erable distance. There were, of course, the examples of na-
ticnal mobilization as effected (but not under the pressure
of war) in Italy, Germany and Russia. Then, too, considerable
information was, no doubt, available concerning the national
mobilization plans of France, Englend and the United States.
Finally, Japan itself had not neglected its own studies and
plans for "national general mobilization".
The stupendous sums involved in the World War were enough
in themselves to cause te Japanese General Staff to con-
sider ways and means to adjust and control the national economy
in such a way as to make the financial burden of warfare
bearable,

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
bearable, to devise plans whereby, with the proper control
under a system or "naticnal general mobilization", it would
be possible to condtct hostilities on the scale required
by modern instruments of war. For without such organization
any considerable effort wes doomed to fail simply because
the nation cculd not be in a position to bear the burden.
One other phase of the program is often popularly called
"taking the profit out of war".
A "military investigation committee" organized during
the World War, therefore, undertook studies not only of
strategic problems, but also of ways and means of effecting
national mobilization in all its aspects. A report on the
latter subject was prepared and submitted to the Government.
This resulted in the passing of the munitions industry
mobilization law of April 16, 19l&, while Count Terauchi
was Prime Minister.* Tne law provided for governmental
control of the munition ināustries and did not touch the
broader problem of "national generel mobilization" as
understood today. But it was a beginning of war-time control
of the natiol's inaustrial r sources. It was a small be-
ginning when compared with the elaborate plans for the
totalitarian, regimented control of all national activities,
not only of Japan but of a great wide area vaguely des-
cribed as the "yen-bloc areas" including the Japanese
Empire, "Manchukuo" and large parts, if not the whole, of
China, which evolvea Irom this start. A brief review of
this evolution may be of some interest.
Under
* Embassy's Despatch No. 102, April 19, 1918.

-3-
Under this law a Bureau of Munitions was established,
the formal organizatien or which was promulgated in June, 1918.
This Bureau, under a directorate comoosed of the Prime Min-
ister as director and the Vice-Ministers for War and Navy
as assistant directors, was to effect cooperation between
the civil overnment authorities and the military and naval
authorities in the application of that part of the law deal-
ing with the cortrol of industry in time of peace.
In January of l9l9, there was promulgated, on the basis
of this law, and Imperial Crdinance concerning a special in-
vestieation of factories and shons for the manufacture of
munitions; and in Dece.ber, 1919, another Imperial Ordinance

ticn of munitins.
The universal aspiration toward peace by a system of
collective secrit foad  , vowever, even in Japan so
that munitions control, investigation, and mobilization plans
had to retreat to the desxs of the military. They were,

This actually represented a step forward
for "national general mopilization" planning, since by
such a move efforts in this direction were no longer
limited to munitions but rightly included a broader field
of general endeavor.

Hith the reforms and clian es, carried out during the

Following the Febru ry 26 Incident the rate of change

Installation of several cabinets cul inating in tnat
under Prince Konoe immediately before the beginning
of the hostilities in China in 19s7 might be said to
mark a definite cliuax in Japanese political affairs
and while no clear line can ever be drawn marking the
end or beginning of a political situation, it represent-
ed the ascendency of political faith in a planned state-
hood for Japan.
That the groundwork of planning and preparing for
such an eventuality had been thorough is evidenced by
the rapidity and completeness with which temporary
control measures were drafted and put into effect.
It has been alleged that the plans for national
general mobilization were taken verbatim from the German
plans. Such an assertion can only be based on ignorance
of the long term studies and investigation which had
been undertaken in Japan. As has been indicated, it
is not at all improbable that the plans of all nations,
so far as available, were carefully studied and the
desired

Hor should their own researches into this cuestion be at

Hational General Mobilization Law was the reolacement of

The " atienel Gener 1 mobilizati n Law"** with its
provisions conveying broad sweeping powers to the Govern-
ment, was devised and ronulgated in order to hasten, in
the face of extraoreinary conditi ns, the very gradual
orocess of coorui..ution and consolidntion which had been
under way ror some time. It is not at all unlikely that
tuose forces in Jap nese polities which see reat value

nation (one in which all forces and resources may be most

situation to obtain from the Diet a virtual relinquishment

gency.

While it ay appear ridiculous to consider a problem
of unemployment as existent in a nation which has large
armies operatin overseas and which is feverishly working
twenty-four hour shifts to supply and equip those armies,
nevertneless, tnere is such a roblen under such circua-
stances in Janan today. het r y choice or by necessity,
Janan has undertaken a drastic alterin or its economy.
The tem or ry restrictions above-mentio ed nave so cur-
tailed and licited the use of practically every material
as to force thousands of inaiviouals to ive up their
customary ursuits. Metals (steel, corper, aluninum, etc.)
are not avai able for making toys, cigarette lighters,
baby automobiles, and any other article not needed by
the military.
Gasoline is rationed - cutting down the number of
truck and bus drivers and practically eliminating t he
pleasure motor boat operators. Import of chemicals is
restricted causing many dyers to sit idle. Amusement
is frowned upon making many entertainers seek other means
of a livelihood. Unemployment exists then because of,
not a lack of demand, but of a revision of the flow
of

-20-
of mate ials and a redirection of effort. There is a
demand for l bor, out labor must be transferred to
other pursuits and directed into new channels. Small
industry (home workshops) is suffering and edding to
this problem of technical unea loynent in the face of
a arent prosperity in many other lines.
The mere existence of hostilities with the re-
sultant excessive strain on the munitions industries,
transportation and shippin, mopilization of en and
all of tie other well kown r sults of waging what
is actually large scale wartare was in itself sufricient
to create an unemployment problem and a maladjustment of
the supply anc demand of labor. It should not be believed
for a moment, lowever, that this has resulted in the type
of unemployment ordinarily . itnessed in an economic de-
pression. Actually many industries are booming in Japan,
labor in these industries is well paid, according to
Japanese standarus. Japan is developing and expanding
its productive ca acity. Heavy metal industries are
being developed, and Jepan will enter a phase of economic
development in which the country will supply to a large
extent its own machines end tools and will eventually
find a market for its surplus in Manchuria and China.
This problem is largely one of facilitating the
transfer of labor from one industry or line of occupa-
tion to another and is not a problem in which there
is an increased surplus of labor. It represents an
aggravation of the ever-present technical unemploy-
ment caused by severe tampering with a functioning
economy.

(a) Spiritual end physical training in the
endurance of harasnips;
(e) Levelo met of a spirit of personal
sacritice;
(f) Com lete raithrulness to individual
auty.

NATIONAL MOBILIZATICI. A.D ITS FUTURE EVOLUTION
Having covered in outli.e the far-reaching emergency
measures and adninistrative chan es which have recently been
effected, it is now pertinent to consider the more permanent
rogram for mobilization and to summarize and comment on the
entire developing situation.
In February of this year, the Government presented its
bill for hati nal movilization to the Diet. There was con-
siderable parliacutary opposition and the interpellations
were, for Ja an, vigorous. The Governlent, at first, was not
strong or convincing in its defense of the proposed measure.
Premier Kono remained at home, ill witn a cold.
The course of this bill through the Diet to its promul-
gation as a law has been fully reported by the Embassy and
reference has been made in this paper* to the various des-
patches on this subject. A careful study of the stenographic
notes of the comnittee meetings in both houses of the Diet
at which the measure was discussed was interesting and some
rather illuminating information was obtained. The inter-
pellators in the comaittee meetings directed their attention
largely to the following points:
1.

-41-
1. The obvicus ract tiut tue law if passed as
drufted cave te the overiment practically
unlimitec le islative powers which should
rigitfully belon only to the Diet. It was
pointed out many times in the committee hear-
ings that almost every article contained the
phrase "........to be cetermined by Imperial
Orainance," thus p-ovidine the Government with
authority to exercise dictatorial power.
The law was said to be contrary to the Imperial
Constituticn.
心.
Such legislation was considered unnecessary in
view or the rovisies of Article 31 of the
Constitution wrien allows com lete freedom of the
Im eriel reroative in time of war or national
ener ency.
Institut'or or such leei lation, it was feared,
woulc be the irst ten toward a type of dictator-

The main defense of the bill eventually rested on Prince
Konoe, the  rime minister. he was, or course, assisted by
the various Cabinet nini,ters and other Government spokesmen.
The Prenier stated to the Peers' Comnittee that the l aw was
the result of  ore than ten years of study and research,
that it is to be the permanent key to national mobilization,
and that it ccutains measures of the greatest importance wen
in ordinary times. He asserted that the provision for legis-
lation by Impelial ordinances was necessary to facilitate quick
action, tc make administrative measures more flexible, and to
protect military secrets. The Government maintained that the
law in no way altered the use of the Imperial prerogative in
emergency times.

1938, the Planning Board of the Cabinet published the Govern-
ment's official attitude toward the bill for national mobili-
zation. In this article it was clearly pointed out that the
demands

demands of modern warfare on any economic system are so

It is important, at this point, to emphasize the

Any study of mobilization in Japan can not be complete
without a reference to such other legislation as that con-
cerning the electric power industry, the North China Develop-
ment Comoany Law, the Central China Promction Company Law, and
various other legislation ror aererian relief, establishment
of a people's credit bank, cploylent a, ency law, currency
laws, et cetera.* Thi reference is mace wit a view to
strengthening the asserticn that mobilization in Janan is
but one part or tie reseit tencency to.ard a system close-
ly resemblin cotaliturianis. in its state control and ra-
tional zation, out beine distinctly Japanese in its forn. A
sin le party may develon, but  dictater is haraly expected
to appear. e "Goverrment" will control, but the Diet will
probebly be c nvered in fulrilimeit of the letter of the con-
stitution. Ja an is ceter.ined by efficiency and unity of
purpose to au ment its national pover. It cannot afford to
co otherwise.
It has been attemrted to give in some detail the process
of political alid cconomic change which is now being undertaken
in Janan, and to provide a minimum of material for an analysis
of that procedure. Japan is engaged in a process of ration-
alization of every national activity and in a regimentation
of her population to the end that there will be a minimun of
waste ffort or material and a maximun return for the efforts
of national energy.
One can not overlook the very serious obstacles and
pitfalls which line the path being pursued. There is un-
employment, there is hardship, and both are increasing.
There is at present nevertheless no indication of any
threat

threat towara rebellion or even active resistance toward
the nat onal policy. The possibility remains, but the

The social conseguences of such a progran are not to
be overlooked: home worksho s are to be eliminated, small
industry suffere, a large lubor class with, undoubtedly, a
labor consciousness will develop (the reat bulk of labor
is today performed by small sroups of men up to a maximum
P0 or 30 per shop). Coupled with this are the amazingly
broad and compliceted roble.s ef tne economic exploitation
of the resorces and po mlati on uf China. It should be
reme .berea that t is is not merely e rogran to facilitate
the c nuuct of hostilities but is an attept to place the
Japanese I ire o. t.e : ost scientiric and rational basis
nossible, to ive the nation the  reate t possible strength and
unity to realize its cesired obj ive of comination and ex-
ploitation of the Far East. This, as Japanese leaders real-
ize, may involve tne active resistance of one or more of the
large Powers, ana Janan is deter ined to be in a position to
exploit to the best advantage every unit of national energy
anc resource.
Various official announcements nave made clear the
probability of the conclusion which a detailed study of
this program produces - it is not to be temporary, it is
a permanent part of a far broader program. The following
is typical of official statements: "Economic control will
continue long after the cessation of hostilities, owing
to the problems of reconstruction in China." The real
struggle will of course come over a long period of time.
Japan

=== Page 325 === (confidence 0.98)

Japan must make each and every expunciture of national or
individual energy yield the sreatest possible return, there-
fore rationalization is to stay.
It must be remembered that such organization, control,
and regimentation i, not ncw to Japan but has in varying
degrees of effectiveuess been evident throughout the entire
histcry of the nation. Nor nas it been necessary for a com-
: lete about-face; in many instances only slight changes, if
any, vere iecessary in existing laws, regulations, or organi-
zation. Leagues, uilds, councils, control and cooperation
even coverin: an entire industry have been and are common in
japan. Upon such a structure it is not difficult to build
a planmed economy to ertect netionel general mobilization or
to rationalize the entire field of national activities.
This fuct stands out; by effecting "national general
mobilizati n" Japan shows its awureness of the present situa-

to carry out its policy in the Far est no matter what any
otner Pover may do or attemet in the way of interference with
that policy, to effect a scientific girding of the body politic
for meeting any eventaality, and to eliminate as far as possible
conflicting opinions or efforts, political and economic.
Whether success of failure results under this reorganized
system, Japan will be as strong as totalitarianism can make
the nation. Once this system is organized and functioning
Japan may, in its own eyes, be ready for new and further con-
quests.

Appendix B
"The Japanese Empire and the Armies of the Great Powers"
ARMY PANPHLET OF JANUARY 1936.
Chapter 6. Preparation for National Mobilization.
Context.
As stated in the preface (omitted), present day national
defense can obtain its object by a demonstration of the entire
national power of the Empire carefully prepared in peace time,
as well as in war time. In future, as in ancient times, the
more highly trained army will conquer its enemy but victory
in the future will be difficult without the perfection of
complete mobilization plans. In our country this preparation
has been desired since l927 but as yet has not reached perfec-
tion.
1.
What National Mobilization Means.
In ancient times when small units were employed, the
supply of equipment was comparatively simple and mobilization
was limited to the army and navy but to last successfully mo-
dern warfare, not only troops are necessary, as mobilization
of the resources of the entire nation also must be carried out.
It may be said thet if adequate preparedness is supplied
the national life is free from threat of war, the spirit is
high and both the military and civilians can endure the con-
tinued pressure of war as one body, with ultimate victory in
the end. This is the necessity for national mobilization,
which is, in other words, a service which will place the entire
national strength of the nation on a wartime footing. All re-
sources, private and public, will be co-ordinated and employed
as economically as possible in a demonstration of the nation's
ability to fight a modern war.
Preparations for national mobilization will be wide in
scope and very difficult, and will consist of guidance of the
national spirit, resunply of insufficient resources, adjust-
ment of the economic system and through preparations for plac-
ing the national resources on a wartime footing.
At present, all the countries in the world are making
desperate efforts to mobilize, especially Soviet Russia which
is sacrificing the every day life of the nation for that pur-
pose, as evidenced by her first and second 5 year programs.
2.
Plan of National Mobilization.
Included in the national mobilization will be spiritual,
personnel, industrial and financial mobilization, communication
control, etc. In the governments of the Great Powers a speci-
al office takes charge of the erecution of mobilization plans,
suoh

=== Page 328 === (confidence 1.00)
-2-
such as France's national defense council, Italy's national
defense committee and our own cabinet resource bureau. The
execution of general mobilization must be accompanied by cer-
tain compulsory measures concerning the rights of the people
and the nation, and a special law will be enacted for that
purpose. Italy has already promulgated general mobilization
and in France, though not yet promulgated, it has once passed
the lower house.
3. Preparation for National Mobilization in Peace Time.
As mentioned above, national mobilization is to prepare
for the eventuality of war but even in peace time it will con-
tribute much toward the development of national resources.
It may be said that the preparations will discover ways for
the supply of deficient raw materials, the consumption of ex-
cessive resources, encourage invention and devise a method for
economic co-ordination in case of an emergency. In short it
will not only contribute to the economic development of the
country in peace time but will acquaint the nation with nation-
al defense and stimulate the patriotism of the people.
4.
Preparations for General Mobilization.
(a) The Organ.
Our country, stimulated by the difficult experiences of
foreign countries durin the Great War, felt the necessity for
preparin for a general mobilization and established the
Munitions Burenu in 19l8. This was combined with the Bureauof
Statistics but later abolished and in its place the Bureau of
National Resources was established in 1927. This is the only
organ in our government which exercises control of general
mobilization. It is under the direct supervision of the Premier
and maintains close contact with all departments, especially the
Army and Navy. Its function is to work out plans for the co-
ordination and employment of all resources in order to meet the
huge wartime demand and thus guarantee national safety.
(b) The Law and Ordinance.
In our country there is no general mobilization law but
the Munition Industrial Mobilization Law (promulgated in 1932),
regulates the direction of munition factories and work shops
and also the induction into service of civilians. This law
covers only munition industry mobilization and is insufficient
as far as general mobilization is concerned.
5.
Outline of our Preparations for General Mobilization.
(a) Spiritual Mobilization.
As mentioned in the preface, in the struggle between two
nations at war, if one country loses the will to fight it is
an infallible sign of victory for the enemy country. The fall
of Russia and the defeat of Germany show that no matter how
strong her army, if a country loses her fighting spirit there
is no other alternative but to sue for peace.
(b)

=== Page 329 === (confidence 1.00)
-3-
(b) Spiritual Mobilization and the battle of Thought.
In future warfare the battle of morale will be carried
out parallel with the battle of arms. Schemes to keep the
spirit of the whole nation solidly behind the troops facing
the enemy will be resorted to, and in order to bear the
heavy pressure of, and gain an ultimate victory in a war of
long duration we must construct an impregnable fortress of
national morale by completing preparations for spiritual
mobilization in peace time and by cultivating the national
will for victory in order to gain ascendency over the enemy.
(c) Spiritual Mobilization in Peace Time.
In every country preperations for national mobiliza-
tion are carried on in time of peace, and this is especially
true of spiritual mobilization since it is very difficult
to arouse national sentiment to the proper pitch at a critical
time.
(d) Pure Japanese Spirit to be Established.
In order to raise the national spirit in peace or war
it is necessary to establish a guiding spirit to lead the
way. It goes without saying that this guiding spirit must
be purely Japanese. It is to be regretted that our national
spirit in which Japan takes so much pride has been slumbering
for some time due to the influence of foreign ideas. Since
the Manchurian incident, however, our spirit has been awakening
and it must be cultivated to create an impregnable spirit which
cannot be buaged an inch by any foreign evil. Reform of
educational institutions, guidance of cultural organizations,
oppression of communists and the proper guidance of liberals
are measures to be taken in the spiritual mobilization. It
is our earnest hope that this can soon be achieved by the
united efforts of the government and the people.
(e) Stabilization of National Life.
It is needless to say that certain fixed standards of
living must be set if the full benefit of spiritual mobilization
is to be aclieved. Japanese fusion will be realized and spirit
heightened by aiding the farming, fishing, and mountain villages
and promoting small commercial and industrial projects,
especially in war time when the national life will be threatened
by food and clothing shortages and enemy air and land attacks,
whereas if the general mobilization plan is completed the
nation will be at ease and pure Japanese spirit will be
high.
6.
Personnel Mobilization.
In time of war the personnel required by the army, navy,
munition factories, etc., will be enormous and even in our
country which is biessed with a large population the supply
of sufficient personnel will be difficult. In a future war
the number of people participating will not be as small as
that during the Russo-Japanese war. Judging from the experi-
ences of the great war the personnel necessary for the muni-
tion factories, dock yards, transportation, etc., is about
double the number of the men in the battle-field, and plans
for the requisition, training and distribution of these men
must

=== Page 330 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
must be made in time of peace.
7.
Conscription Reform.
In time of war mobilization will include men and women,
young and old, -- in short anyone capable of employment, and
a special consaiption department must be established to
handle the recruiting of the personnel and the distribution
of labor. Men cualified for, or engaged in military service
will be temporarily exempted if they are needed for technical
labor in munition factcries, workshops, etc., which will be
under government supervision.
8. Spirit of Service and Equalization of Burden.
It is absolutely necessary for the national defense that
all the people voluntarily engaged in national enterprises in
time of crisis, that they willingly bear their share of the
burden and receive pay commensurate with their work. This is
one of the aims to be achieved by spiritual mobilization.
9.
Industrial Mobilization.
Industry is to be co-ordinated during peace time so that
it can handle munitions supply during war time, and if this
change cennot be accomplished smoothly it will bring on an
economic penic, war supply will be impossible, and the fight-
ing spirit of the nation will be destroyed; therefore, it is
necessary to lay the founuations for war time supply during
times of peace.
10. Supplementing Deficiences in War Materials.
There are meny important commodities which are not among
the resources of our country, such as oil, rubber, etc., and
in case of an emergency these must be used as economically as
possible, and substitutes devised. Concerning oil and rubber,
these coimodities cannot be obtained in sufficient quantities
from countries within our sphere of influence and the supply
must be achieved by imports. Since in war purchases abroad
may be limited to within our sphere of influence and that of
our allies, we must in peace time expand our trade in prepara-
tion for an emergency always in our minds.
1l. Priority of Munitions.
In order that military operations may not suffer, a suf-
ficient supply of munitions will be the first concern in the
consideration of resources. Although the military authorities
will economize as much as possible, all materials in our
country which can be used for munitions will be given without
stint and the people will be forced to use substitutes.
12. Consumption of Excess Resources.
Increase of consumptive power must be undertaken.
13. Factory Mobilization.
Expansion of plants and factories, government control,
and standardization of production of service equipment will
be undertaken.
14.

=== Page 331 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
14。
Financial Mobilization.
Jar expenditure differs with the time and scale of the
war and it is estimated that during the European War Germany's
yearly expenditure was 34,100,000,000 marks, England's 1,750,-
000,000 pounds and iAmerica's 13,100,000,000 dollars. In the
future our war expenditure will not be as small as that
during the Russo-Japanese War at which time the total expendi-
ture was only 1,700,000,000 yen. Plans for raising the money
necessary to carry on modern warfare must be perfected.
15. Raising of Finances for Var Expenditure.
Funds for wer expenditure will be raised by increasing
taxes on goveriment monopolies, increase of general taxes,
issuance of bonds and all other possible methods. The first
two methods mentioned above will bring in a steady income
but it will not be sufficient and the remainder will undoubtedly
have to be raised by issuing bonds. Here the question is
whether they will be issued to the public or underwritten by
the central banks though this latter method is usual. Issu-
ance of paper money not bacled by silver currency is another
possibility but this must be limited or the result will bring
inflation of currency and en economic panic.
16.
Other Mobilization Aims.
Other subjects for mobilizetion are co-ordination of
communication, air, sea snd land transportation, propaganda
and intelligent enterprises and enactment of laws to handle
general mobilization.

reached a definite climax with the announcement by the
Cabinet

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.97)

a

2114

January 4, 1939.
Baron Kiichiro Hiranuma is 71 years old. Following
a long career in the legal field, he became Minister of
Justice in 1923, a member of the Privy Council in 1924,
Vice President of the Privy Council in 1926, and President
in March, 1936.
Founder of one atriotic reactionary society and
leader of another, he is popular with the rightest elements
F
and unpopular with the moderates. The insurgents,
responsible for the uprising of February, 1936, demanded
894.00/829
 ren Hiranuma's ap ointment as I remier (or that of Admiral
I m i Yato), presumably because he had ong been known as
a chauvinist and an unti-moderate. Wis appointment as
'resident of the : rivy Council in March, 1936, was
presu.ably in some measure due to his influence in military
and chauvinist circles.

JAN1:1939
PA/A:
Mr. Hormbeck.
Tokyo's despatch No. 3549 of December 23,
1938, entitled "The'Asia Promotion Board
(Koa-in)", reports the promulgation of
Imperial Ordinances on December 16 to create
that board to function "during the China
Incident", for the purpose of administering
all affairs connected with China. The
despatch (4 pages) outlines briefly the scope
of the board, refers to Tokyo's despatch No.
3300 of Octoher 4 (attached hereto) for con-
nent on the board, points out the extensive
preparations for the establishment of
"affiliated offices" in China and the strong
position held by the military in relation to
the Asia Promotion Board, as evidenced by the
appointment of military officers to important
positions, states that it is too early to
predict to what extent supervision by the
Cabinet will be effective or to what degree
the scope of the board may be broadened, and
suggests that the board will exist until the
program for the reconstruction and development
of China and the economic, political and
cultural cooperation of Japan, "Manchukuo"
and China has been effected.
FE:Salisbury:REK

With reference to my despatch no. 3300, October 4,
1938, I have the honor to report that the Koa-in (Asia
Promotion Board), which is the nane finally decided upon
for the central orcan establisied to administer all affairs
F/A
connected with Chine, has been officially organized.
1/2/3
There are enclosed for the Department's information the
Embassy's translations of the Imperial Crdinances for the
official

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.99)
6299
-2-
official organization of the Asia Promotion Board and
for the official organization of the affiliated offioos as
well as a list of the officials appointed.
The Board, organized under the presidency of the
premier, is to administer during the China incident,
with the specified exce tion of diplomatic matters, the
following affairs:
1. Political, economic and cultural affairs
which, in connection with the China incident
must be managed in China.
Matters having to do with the establishment of
policies in connection with the above affairs.
3.
Affairs relating to the supervision of the
North China Development Company and the Central
China Fromotion Company and to the control of
Japanese businessmen in China.
4.
Affairs related to the maintenance of unity
among Government offices in their relations
with China.
The vice presidents are specified in the orainance to
be the Ministers for Forei n Affairs, War, Navy, and Finance.
A director general, to be accorded the treatment of an offi-
cial of shinnin rank, is to be the general administrative
officer. General Yanagawa has been appointed to this im-
portant position.
The general organization of this board largely conforms
to that of former projected organizations which have been
fully reported to the Department by various despatches,
particularly my no. 3300, October 4, and there is little
additional comment which can be made at this time. It may
be, however, of some si nificance to point out the extensive
proparations for the establ shment of "affiliated offices"
in

=== Page 376 === (confidence 0.98)
6300
-3-
in China, a liaison committee for the purpose of
facilitating unity in the policies of the various
Government o fices regarding China and the provision
for the participation in the work of the Asia Promotion
Board by Army and Mavy officers on the active list,
such officers to retain their active service standing.
The strong position held by the military in relation
to the Asia Board is evidenced by the appointment of
General Yanagawa, General Juzuki, and other military
officers to important positions. It is yet too early
to predict to whet extent the supervision by the Cabinet
will be effective, or whether the scope of this Board
may be broadened until it equels or surpasses a ministry
in importance.
The progress in the or anization of this central
organ has been very slow, however, during the latter
part of November, it ws announced that the Cabinet
hed finally anproved the proposed organization of an
official organ for the control of relations with Chine
and that the name to be adopted would be the Koa-in
(Asia Fromotion Eoard). On Decenber 2, it was reported
that the Privy Council nad approved the draft plan, but
at the same time had attached a statement of "its hopes"
to the effect that the greatest consideration be given
to the functioning of the Board, the selection of its
personnel, and the relations between the Board and
"local

=== Page 377 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
"local agencies", and further that every care be taken
to avoid friction with the diplomatio authoritios. The
.Committee's approval was followed shortly thereafter
by the formal approval of the Privy Council and the
Imperial Ordinances were promulgated on December 16.
The Board reportedly began functioning immediately.
While the Imperial Ordinance states that the Board is
to function "during the China Incident", a Government
spokesman reportedly stated in reply to a question posed
by the Privy Council Committee that the end of the China
incident will be fixed by Imperial decision and that the
Asia Promotion Soara may exist even if the emergenoy is
brou ht to an end. It is also obvious from various press
reports of Government statements that the emergency will
not be cousidered to have ended with the termination of
active hostilities but that the necessity for such organi-
zations as the asia Promotion Board will continue until
Japan's policies and prograns for a new order in East
Asia have been realized. This has been interpreted by
Government spokesmen to mean until the program for the
reconstruction and develop ent of China and the economic,
political, ana cultural cooperation of Japan, "Manchukuo"
and China has been effected.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

Board organized under the direction of the Prime Minister,
shall administer the followi g affairs with the exception,
however, of those affairs related to diplomacy.
1. Political, economic, and cultural affairs which,
in connection with the China incident, must be
managed in China.

offices.

Article 3. In addition to the officials listed in the
foregoing article, executive secretaries may be appointed
by the Cabinet, after nomination by the Prime Minister to
the Emperor, from among higher civil service officials
of Government offices concerned.

Article 4. The Asia Promotion Board shall have the
President's Secretariat and the following three depart-
ments:
Political Department
Economic Department
Cultural Department.
The Asia Promotion Board may also have a technical
department. When this department is organized, the ofrice
of the chief of the department shall be filled by an engineer
of chokunin rank.
Assignment of the work among the President's secretariat
and various departments shall be determined by the Prime
Minister.
Article 5. A liaison committee shall be organized and
attached to the Asia Promotion Board for the purpose of carry-
ing out the work of correlating the affairs of the various
Governmental offices concerned in regard to important matters
mentioned in Article 1.
This liaison Committee shell be composed of one Chairman
and several committee members. The office of Chairman shall
be held by the Director General, and the committee members
shall be nominated to the Throne by the Prime Minister from
among higher civil service officials of the Government offices
concerned and shall be appointed by the Cabinet.
The liaison committee shall have an executive secretary
who shall be nominated to the Throne by the Prime Minister
from among higher civil service officials of Government
offices concerned, and shall be appointed·by the Cabinet.
The executive secretary shall, under the orders of higher
officials, superintend general affairs.
